Loaf of White Bread, 1930-2024 JAH-COTY-000001 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A loaf of white bread is the oldest and simplest item in the American price record: the Bureau of Labor Statistics has priced a pound loaf of white pan bread in United States cities for over a century, and the series runs unbroken from the Great Depression to the present. This record gives the average retail price of a one-pound loaf of white bread for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with each figure sourced, and converts every price into 2024 dollars so the reader can see whether bread genuinely became dearer or only kept pace with inflation. Bread is the natural yardstick for the whole database: nearly everyone bought it, its quality changed slowly, and its price history tracks the grain market, the bakery trade, and the general price level at once.

History

White pan bread was one of the first foods the Bureau of Labor Statistics priced for its cost-of-living work, because bread was the daily staple of nearly every household. In 1930 a loaf cost nine cents, in the middle of the Depression, when bakers competed on price and many families baked at home. The price barely moved through the 1930s and 1940s, held down by cheap wheat and by wartime price controls, and it was still only fourteen cents in 1950. The 1960s and 1970s brought the first real climbs, as wheat prices rose and bakery wages grew, and the loaf passed fifty cents in 1980. The 2000s added the wheat spikes of 2008 and the grain shock of 2022, and by 2024 the average loaf cost $1.97, more than twenty times its 1930 price in nominal terms but only about twice its 1930 price in 2024 dollars.

Description

A loaf of white bread is the oldest and simplest item in the American price record: the Bureau of Labor Statistics has priced a pound loaf of white pan bread in United States cities for over a century, and the series runs unbroken from the Great Depression to the present. This record gives the average retail price of a one-pound loaf of white bread for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with each figure sourced, and converts every price into 2024 dollars so the reader can see whether bread genuinely became dearer or only kept pace with inflation. Bread is the natural yardstick for the whole database: nearly everyone bought it, its quality changed slowly, and its price history tracks the grain market, the bakery trade, and the general price level at once.

The Problem

What did a loaf of white bread cost in each decade from 1930 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and how much of the rise was general inflation rather than bread becoming genuinely dearer?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average retail price of white pan bread per pound from the Stacker compilation of BLS average prices, cross-checked against the BLS Bulletin 1055 figures for 1950. Step 2: record one anchor per decade, 1930 through 2024. Step 3: for each anchor, divide by the CPI-U annual average of its year and multiply by the CPI-U annual average of 2024 (313.7) to get the price in 2024 dollars. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and the compound annual growth rate over the whole span, and report both. The series is 1930: $0.09 per lb; 1940: $0.08 per lb; 1950: $0.14 per lb; 1960: $0.20 per lb; 1970: $0.24 per lb; 1980: $0.51 per lb; 1990: $0.69 per lb; 2000: $0.93 per lb; 2010: $1.37 per lb; 2020: $1.45 per lb; 2024: $1.97 per lb. The series has no missing decade anchors. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 11, "earliest_year": 1930, "latest_year": 2024, "per_lb_first": 0.09, "per_lb_last": 1.97, "per_lb_multiple": 21.89, "per_lb_cagr": 0.0334}.

The Answer

The loaf cost $0.09 in 1930 and $1.97 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1930 loaf cost about $1.69, so the real price roughly doubled over ninety-four years while the nominal price rose more than twentyfold. The fastest real rise came after 2000, when the loaf went from $0.93 to $1.97 while general prices rose far less. The series is 1930: $0.09 per lb; 1940: $0.08 per lb; 1950: $0.14 per lb; 1960: $0.20 per lb; 1970: $0.24 per lb; 1980: $0.51 per lb; 1990: $0.69 per lb; 2000: $0.93 per lb; 2010: $1.37 per lb; 2020: $1.45 per lb; 2024: $1.97 per lb. From 1930 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.09 to $1.97, a multiple of 21.89 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.34% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

The series has no missing decade anchors. The BLS loaf is a standard white pan loaf, not an artisan or whole-grain loaf, and the mix of breads Americans buy has shifted toward more expensive varieties, so the series understates what many shoppers actually spend on bread today. The 1930 and 1940 figures come from the early BLS retail-price program, whose city coverage was narrower than the modern survey. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1930 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.09 to $1.97, a multiple of 21.89 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.34% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Bread prices follow the wheat market with a lag: wheat is milled, baked, packaged, and trucked, and each step adds labor and energy costs that move with the general price level. That is why bread tracked the CPI-U closely for most of the century and pulled ahead of it only when grain prices spiked, as in 2008 and 2022. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

AI

The JAH Cost of Things Librarian answers questions about this record, explains the reasoning, and runs the demo and program on request.

Specification

  • dimensions: price in US dollars per per lb; calendar years 1930-2024; CPI-U index points
  • care: Never present a gap year as a price; never mix units across a unit change; always state the unit and the year with every figure.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One loaf of white bread in one year: what it cost and what that meant.
  • A2: The whole decade series: the price across the decades of American shopping.
  • A3: The price against the whole economy: nominal and 2024 dollars side by side.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.09, 'per lb'), (1940, 0.08, 'per lb'), (1950, 0.14, 'per lb'), (1960, 0.2, 'per lb'), (1970, 0.24, 'per lb'), (1980, 0.51, 'per lb'), (1990, 0.69, 'per lb'), (2000, 0.93, 'per lb'), (2010, 1.37, 'per lb'), (2020, 1.45, 'per lb'), (2024, 1.97, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class BreadPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1930, 0.09, 'per lb'), (1940, 0.08, 'per lb'), (1950, 0.14, 'per lb'), (1960, 0.2, 'per lb'), (1970, 0.24, 'per lb'), (1980, 0.51, 'per lb'), (1990, 0.69, 'per lb'), (2000, 0.93, 'per lb'), (2010, 1.37, 'per lb'), (2020, 1.45, 'per lb'), (2024, 1.97, 'per lb')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(BreadPriceProgram().run())

Gallon of Whole Milk, 1930-2024 JAH-COTY-000002 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

Whole milk is the most-bought grocery in the American record, and its price series is also one of the trickiest, because the unit changes halfway through: the published figures are per half-gallon through 1990 and per gallon from 2000 onward. This record gives the average retail price of whole milk for every decade from 1930 to 2024, keeps the two units strictly apart, and converts every price into 2024 dollars within its own unit. Milk tracks the dairy economy: feed costs, the milk marketing orders, the shift from home delivery to the supermarket, and the consolidation of dairy farming.

History

In 1930 milk arrived at the door in glass bottles, priced by the half-gallon at twenty-eight cents, and the milkman was a fixture of American mornings. Home delivery held through the 1950s, when the half-gallon cost forty-one cents, and faded in the 1960s and 1970s as supermarkets took over and the gallon jug replaced the bottle. The published series switches to the gallon in the late 1990s: $2.70 in 1998 and $2.84 in 1999, then $2.78 in 2000. Prices stayed near three dollars a gallon through the 2010s, then climbed with feed and transport costs to $3.98 in 2024.

Description

Whole milk is the most-bought grocery in the American record, and its price series is also one of the trickiest, because the unit changes halfway through: the published figures are per half-gallon through 1990 and per gallon from 2000 onward. This record gives the average retail price of whole milk for every decade from 1930 to 2024, keeps the two units strictly apart, and converts every price into 2024 dollars within its own unit. Milk tracks the dairy economy: feed costs, the milk marketing orders, the shift from home delivery to the supermarket, and the consolidation of dairy farming.

The Problem

What did whole milk cost in each decade from 1930 to 2024, and how do the half-gallon decades compare with the gallon decades without mixing the two units?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average retail price of fresh milk from the Stacker compilation of BLS average prices. Step 2: record one anchor per decade, keeping the published unit for each year: per half-gallon for 1930 through 1990, per gallon for 2000 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars within its own unit using the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate separately for the half-gallon span (1930-1990) and the gallon span (2000-2024), and never compute a multiple across the unit change. The series is 1930: $0.28 per half-gallon; 1940: $0.26 per half-gallon; 1950: $0.41 per half-gallon; 1960: $0.52 per half-gallon; 1970: $0.66 per half-gallon; 1980: $1.05 per half-gallon; 1990: $1.42 per half-gallon; 2000: $2.78 per gallon; 2010: $3.26 per gallon; 2020: $3.32 per gallon; 2024: $3.98 per gallon. The unit changes in the published series: 1930 through 1990 are per half-gallon (delivered milk in the early decades, grocery-store half-gallons later), and 2000 onward is per gallon, so the demo computes multiples only within each unit, never across the change. Gaps stated, not filled: BLS published no average retail food prices from July 1978 to December 1979 during the CPI revision, so the series jumps from the 1970s to 1980. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 11, "earliest_year": 1930, "latest_year": 2024, "per_half_gallon_first": 0.28, "per_half_gallon_last": 1.42, "per_half_gallon_multiple": 5.07, "per_half_gallon_cagr": 0.0274, "per_gallon_first": 2.78, "per_gallon_last": 3.98, "per_gallon_multiple": 1.43, "per_gallon_cagr": 0.0151}.

The Answer

The half-gallon cost $0.28 in 1930 and $1.42 in 1990, a multiple of about 5.1 over sixty years. The gallon cost $2.78 in 2000 and $3.98 in 2024, a multiple of about 1.4 over twenty-four years. In 2024 dollars the 1930 half-gallon cost about $5.26, which is far more than the 2024 half-gallon equivalent of about $1.99, so milk became genuinely cheaper in real terms over the century, as dairy farming industrialized. The series is 1930: $0.28 per half-gallon; 1940: $0.26 per half-gallon; 1950: $0.41 per half-gallon; 1960: $0.52 per half-gallon; 1970: $0.66 per half-gallon; 1980: $1.05 per half-gallon; 1990: $1.42 per half-gallon; 2000: $2.78 per gallon; 2010: $3.26 per gallon; 2020: $3.32 per gallon; 2024: $3.98 per gallon. From 1930 to 1990 the per half-gallon price rose from $0.28 to $1.42, a multiple of 5.07 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.74% per year. From 2000 to 2024 the per gallon price rose from $2.78 to $3.98, a multiple of 1.43 and a compound annual growth rate of 1.51% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: BLS published no average retail food prices from July 1978 to December 1979 during the CPI revision, so the series jumps from the 1970s to 1980. The early figures are delivered milk, the later figures are supermarket milk, and delivery carried a service premium, so part of the apparent real decline is a change in what was bought, not only a fall in price. The 1974-1997 half-gallon figures are grocery-store prices, a different channel again. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1930 to 1990 the per half-gallon price rose from $0.28 to $1.42, a multiple of 5.07 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.74% per year. From 2000 to 2024 the per gallon price rose from $2.78 to $3.98, a multiple of 1.43 and a compound annual growth rate of 1.51% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Milk prices follow feed-grain prices, energy costs for refrigeration and trucking, and the federal milk marketing orders that set minimum prices paid to farmers. The long real decline reflects enormous productivity gains: a dairy cow in 2024 gives roughly three times the milk of a cow in 1930, and bulk tankers replaced the milkman. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

History

In 1930 milk arrived at the door in glass bottles, priced by the half-gallon at twenty-eight cents, and the milkman was a fixture of American mornings. Home delivery held through the 1950s, when the half-gallon cost forty-one cents, and faded in the 1960s and 1970s as supermarkets took over and the gallon jug replaced the bottle. The published series switches to the gallon in the late 1990s: $2.70 in 1998 and $2.84 in 1999, then $2.78 in 2000. Prices stayed near three dollars a gallon through the 2010s, then climbed with feed and transport costs to $3.98 in 2024.

Problem

What did whole milk cost in each decade from 1930 to 2024, and how do the half-gallon decades compare with the gallon decades without mixing the two units?

Method

Step 1: take the BLS average retail price of fresh milk from the Stacker compilation of BLS average prices. Step 2: record one anchor per decade, keeping the published unit for each year: per half-gallon for 1930 through 1990, per gallon for 2000 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars within its own unit using the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate separately for the half-gallon span (1930-1990) and the gallon span (2000-2024), and never compute a multiple across the unit change. The series is 1930: $0.28 per half-gallon; 1940: $0.26 per half-gallon; 1950: $0.41 per half-gallon; 1960: $0.52 per half-gallon; 1970: $0.66 per half-gallon; 1980: $1.05 per half-gallon; 1990: $1.42 per half-gallon; 2000: $2.78 per gallon; 2010: $3.26 per gallon; 2020: $3.32 per gallon; 2024: $3.98 per gallon. The unit changes in the published series: 1930 through 1990 are per half-gallon (delivered milk in the early decades, grocery-store half-gallons later), and 2000 onward is per gallon, so the demo computes multiples only within each unit, never across the change. Gaps stated, not filled: BLS published no average retail food prices from July 1978 to December 1979 during the CPI revision, so the series jumps from the 1970s to 1980. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars.

Dataset

BLS average retail prices and CPI-U annual averages as cited; series years 1930-2024; CPI-U 2024 annual average 313.7.

Skeptical analysis

Gaps stated, not filled: BLS published no average retail food prices from July 1978 to December 1979 during the CPI revision, so the series jumps from the 1970s to 1980. The early figures are delivered milk, the later figures are supermarket milk, and delivery carried a service premium, so part of the apparent real decline is a change in what was bought, not only a fall in price. The 1974-1997 half-gallon figures are grocery-store prices, a different channel again. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.28, 'per half-gallon'), (1940, 0.26, 'per half-gallon'), (1950, 0.41, 'per half-gallon'), (1960, 0.52, 'per half-gallon'), (1970, 0.66, 'per half-gallon'), (1980, 1.05, 'per half-gallon'), (1990, 1.42, 'per half-gallon'), (2000, 2.78, 'per gallon'), (2010, 3.26, 'per gallon'), (2020, 3.32, 'per gallon'), (2024, 3.98, 'per gallon')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class MilkPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1930, 0.28, 'per half-gallon'), (1940, 0.26, 'per half-gallon'), (1950, 0.41, 'per half-gallon'), (1960, 0.52, 'per half-gallon'), (1970, 0.66, 'per half-gallon'), (1980, 1.05, 'per half-gallon'), (1990, 1.42, 'per half-gallon'), (2000, 2.78, 'per gallon'), (2010, 3.26, 'per gallon'), (2020, 3.32, 'per gallon'), (2024, 3.98, 'per gallon')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(MilkPriceProgram().run())

Dozen Eggs, 1930-2024 JAH-COTY-000003 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A dozen large eggs is the most volatile staple in the American price record: egg prices swing with feed costs, flock size, and disease, and they have produced the sharpest single-year jumps of any grocery in the 2020s. This record gives the average retail price of a dozen eggs for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with each figure sourced from the BLS average-price series, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Eggs are the database's best lesson in why a decade anchor can hide the drama: the 2022 average of $2.86 and the 2023 average of $2.80 sit between a 2020 average of $1.51 and a 2024 average of $3.17, and the monthly peaks ran higher still.

History

Eggs cost forty-five cents a dozen in 1930, fell to thirty-three cents in the Depression year 1940, and stayed under a dollar a dozen until the 1990s, when the average crossed $1.01. The price was remarkably flat for decades because egg production industrialized early: battery cages, formulated feed, and year-round laying kept supply steady. The 2010s broke the pattern. Avian influenza cut flocks in 2015, pushing the average to $2.47, and the outbreak of 2022 pushed it to $2.86, the largest one-year jump in the series. Bird flu returned late in 2024, and the average reached $3.17, the highest in the record.

Description

A dozen large eggs is the most volatile staple in the American price record: egg prices swing with feed costs, flock size, and disease, and they have produced the sharpest single-year jumps of any grocery in the 2020s. This record gives the average retail price of a dozen eggs for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with each figure sourced from the BLS average-price series, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Eggs are the database's best lesson in why a decade anchor can hide the drama: the 2022 average of $2.86 and the 2023 average of $2.80 sit between a 2020 average of $1.51 and a 2024 average of $3.17, and the monthly peaks ran higher still.

The Problem

What did a dozen eggs cost in each decade from 1930 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and how much of the recent jump is disease and feed costs rather than general inflation?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average retail price of fresh eggs per dozen from the Stacker compilation of BLS average prices, cross-checked against the MealFan grocery index. Step 2: record one anchor per decade, 1930 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span, and note the 2015, 2022, and 2024 disease years in the record. The series is 1930: $0.45 per dozen; 1940: $0.33 per dozen; 1950: $0.60 per dozen; 1960: $0.57 per dozen; 1970: $0.61 per dozen; 1980: $0.84 per dozen; 1990: $1.01 per dozen; 2000: $0.91 per dozen; 2010: $1.66 per dozen; 2020: $1.51 per dozen; 2024: $3.17 per dozen. The series has no missing decade anchors. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 11, "earliest_year": 1930, "latest_year": 2024, "per_dozen_first": 0.45, "per_dozen_last": 3.17, "per_dozen_multiple": 7.04, "per_dozen_cagr": 0.021}.

The Answer

Eggs cost $0.45 a dozen in 1930 and $3.17 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1930 dozen cost about $8.45, so eggs became much cheaper in real terms over the century, even after the 2020s jumps. The 2024 price is more than double the 2020 price of $1.51, the sharpest four-year rise in the series. The series is 1930: $0.45 per dozen; 1940: $0.33 per dozen; 1950: $0.60 per dozen; 1960: $0.57 per dozen; 1970: $0.61 per dozen; 1980: $0.84 per dozen; 1990: $1.01 per dozen; 2000: $0.91 per dozen; 2010: $1.66 per dozen; 2020: $1.51 per dozen; 2024: $3.17 per dozen. From 1930 to 2024 the per dozen price rose from $0.45 to $3.17, a multiple of 7.04 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.1% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

The series has no missing decade anchors. The BLS dozen is Grade A large eggs, the standard supermarket item; free-range, organic, and pasture-raised eggs cost far more and are not in this series. Monthly prices in 2022 and 2024 ran well above the annual averages quoted here, so the decade anchors understate the worst months. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1930 to 2024 the per dozen price rose from $0.45 to $3.17, a multiple of 7.04 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.1% per year. Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Egg prices follow corn and soybean prices, because feed is most of the cost of an egg, and flock size, because a laying hen takes months to replace. Avian influenza removes millions of hens at once, so supply falls faster than demand and the price spikes until flocks are rebuilt. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100.   • MealFan - Grocery Price Index 2026 (BLS Average Price Data) — https://www.mealfan.com/grocery-price-index/ (BLS average price data for eggs, January 2020 and October 2026 points.)
  • Taste of Home - This Is What Groceries Cost the Year You Were Born — https://www.tasteofhome.com/collection/this-is-what-groceries-cost-the-year-you-were-born/ (Grocery prices by birth year, cross-checking the BLS egg series.)

History

Eggs cost forty-five cents a dozen in 1930, fell to thirty-three cents in the Depression year 1940, and stayed under a dollar a dozen until the 1990s, when the average crossed $1.01. The price was remarkably flat for decades because egg production industrialized early: battery cages, formulated feed, and year-round laying kept supply steady. The 2010s broke the pattern. Avian influenza cut flocks in 2015, pushing the average to $2.47, and the outbreak of 2022 pushed it to $2.86, the largest one-year jump in the series. Bird flu returned late in 2024, and the average reached $3.17, the highest in the record.

Problem

What did a dozen eggs cost in each decade from 1930 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and how much of the recent jump is disease and feed costs rather than general inflation?

Method

Step 1: take the BLS average retail price of fresh eggs per dozen from the Stacker compilation of BLS average prices, cross-checked against the MealFan grocery index. Step 2: record one anchor per decade, 1930 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span, and note the 2015, 2022, and 2024 disease years in the record. The series is 1930: $0.45 per dozen; 1940: $0.33 per dozen; 1950: $0.60 per dozen; 1960: $0.57 per dozen; 1970: $0.61 per dozen; 1980: $0.84 per dozen; 1990: $1.01 per dozen; 2000: $0.91 per dozen; 2010: $1.66 per dozen; 2020: $1.51 per dozen; 2024: $3.17 per dozen. The series has no missing decade anchors. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars.

Dataset

BLS average retail prices and CPI-U annual averages as cited; series years 1930-2024; CPI-U 2024 annual average 313.7.

Verification path

Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Formal equation

P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1

Formal equation, in plain words

P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

Eggs cost $0.45 a dozen in 1930 and $3.17 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1930 dozen cost about $8.45, so eggs became much cheaper in real terms over the century, even after the 2020s jumps. The 2024 price is more than double the 2020 price of $1.51, the sharpest four-year rise in the series. The series is 1930: $0.45 per dozen; 1940: $0.33 per dozen; 1950: $0.60 per dozen; 1960: $0.57 per dozen; 1970: $0.61 per dozen; 1980: $0.84 per dozen; 1990: $1.01 per dozen; 2000: $0.91 per dozen; 2010: $1.66 per dozen; 2020: $1.51 per dozen; 2024: $3.17 per dozen. From 1930 to 2024 the per dozen price rose from $0.45 to $3.17, a multiple of 7.04 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.1% per year.

Skeptical analysis

The series has no missing decade anchors. The BLS dozen is Grade A large eggs, the standard supermarket item; free-range, organic, and pasture-raised eggs cost far more and are not in this series. Monthly prices in 2022 and 2024 ran well above the annual averages quoted here, so the decade anchors understate the worst months. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Specification

  • dimensions: price in US dollars per per dozen; calendar years 1930-2024; CPI-U index points
  • care: Never present a gap year as a price; never mix units across a unit change; always state the unit and the year with every figure.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One dozen eggs in one year: what it cost and what that meant.
  • A2: The whole decade series: the price across the decades of American shopping.
  • A3: The price against the whole economy: nominal and 2024 dollars side by side.

Key figures

  • points: 11
  • earliest_year: 1930
  • latest_year: 2024
  • per_dozen_first: 0.45
  • per_dozen_last: 3.17
  • per_dozen_multiple: 7.04
  • per_dozen_cagr: 0.021

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.45, 'per dozen'), (1940, 0.33, 'per dozen'), (1950, 0.6, 'per dozen'), (1960, 0.57, 'per dozen'), (1970, 0.61, 'per dozen'), (1980, 0.84, 'per dozen'), (1990, 1.01, 'per dozen'), (2000, 0.91, 'per dozen'), (2010, 1.66, 'per dozen'), (2020, 1.51, 'per dozen'), (2024, 3.17, 'per dozen')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class EggsPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1930, 0.45, 'per dozen'), (1940, 0.33, 'per dozen'), (1950, 0.6, 'per dozen'), (1960, 0.57, 'per dozen'), (1970, 0.61, 'per dozen'), (1980, 0.84, 'per dozen'), (1990, 1.01, 'per dozen'), (2000, 0.91, 'per dozen'), (2010, 1.66, 'per dozen'), (2020, 1.51, 'per dozen'), (2024, 3.17, 'per dozen')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(EggsPriceProgram().run())

Gallon of Regular Gasoline, 1930-2024 JAH-COTY-000004 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A gallon of regular gasoline is the most-watched price in America, posted on signs on every highway, and its history is the history of oil shocks, wars, and the American car. This record gives the average United States retail price of a gallon of regular gasoline for every decade from 1930 to 2024, sourced from the Department of Energy and Energy Information Administration compilations, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Gasoline is the database's best example of a price that falls in real terms for decades and then jumps: it cost twenty cents in 1930, eighteen cents in 1940, and only $1.51 in 2000, before the 2000s oil boom and the 2022 shock carried it to $3.30 in 2024.

History

Gasoline was cheap for the first forty years of the series because American oil was plentiful and the federal gasoline tax was small: twenty cents a gallon in 1930, eighteen cents in 1940, twenty-seven cents in 1950, thirty-one cents in 1960, and thirty-six cents in 1970. The 1973 oil embargo and the 1979 revolution in Iran broke that pattern, and the price passed a dollar in 1980 at $1.19. Prices fell back in real terms through the 1980s and 1990s, reaching $1.51 in 2000, then climbed with the oil boom to $2.79 in 2010, fell to $2.17 in the pandemic year 2020, spiked to $3.95 in 2022 after the invasion of Ukraine, and settled at $3.30 in 2024.

Description

A gallon of regular gasoline is the most-watched price in America, posted on signs on every highway, and its history is the history of oil shocks, wars, and the American car. This record gives the average United States retail price of a gallon of regular gasoline for every decade from 1930 to 2024, sourced from the Department of Energy and Energy Information Administration compilations, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Gasoline is the database's best example of a price that falls in real terms for decades and then jumps: it cost twenty cents in 1930, eighteen cents in 1940, and only $1.51 in 2000, before the 2000s oil boom and the 2022 shock carried it to $3.30 in 2024.

The Problem

What did a gallon of regular gasoline cost in each decade from 1930 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was gasoline genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1930 once inflation is removed?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the average retail price of regular gasoline per gallon from the Department of Energy and Energy Information Administration historical compilations as reported by CreditDonkey and TrueDrivingCost, with the 2024 figure from the EIA annual average reported by Gas Prices by State and Rigzone. Step 2: record one anchor per decade, 1930 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1930: $0.20 per gallon; 1940: $0.18 per gallon; 1950: $0.27 per gallon; 1960: $0.31 per gallon; 1970: $0.36 per gallon; 1980: $1.19 per gallon; 1990: $1.15 per gallon; 2000: $1.51 per gallon; 2010: $2.79 per gallon; 2020: $2.17 per gallon; 2024: $3.30 per gallon. Gaps stated, not filled: the EIA annual series starts in 1992, so the 1930-1990 figures come from the DOE/EIA historical compilations cited, not from a single continuous EIA annual table. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 11, "earliest_year": 1930, "latest_year": 2024, "per_gallon_first": 0.2, "per_gallon_last": 3.3, "per_gallon_multiple": 16.5, "per_gallon_cagr": 0.0303}.

The Answer

Gasoline cost $0.20 a gallon in 1930 and $3.30 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1930 gallon cost about $3.76, so gasoline in 2024 was actually slightly cheaper in real terms than in 1930, despite the nominal price rising more than sixteenfold. The real peak was 1980, when $1.19 a gallon equaled about $4.53 in 2024 dollars. The series is 1930: $0.20 per gallon; 1940: $0.18 per gallon; 1950: $0.27 per gallon; 1960: $0.31 per gallon; 1970: $0.36 per gallon; 1980: $1.19 per gallon; 1990: $1.15 per gallon; 2000: $1.51 per gallon; 2010: $2.79 per gallon; 2020: $2.17 per gallon; 2024: $3.30 per gallon. From 1930 to 2024 the per gallon price rose from $0.20 to $3.30, a multiple of 16.5 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.03% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: the EIA annual series starts in 1992, so the 1930-1990 figures come from the DOE/EIA historical compilations cited, not from a single continuous EIA annual table. The early figures are national averages compiled from state and industry records, less precise than the modern EIA weekly survey, and the modern EIA annual average covers regular grade only; premium and diesel prices run higher. State taxes differ by more than a dollar a gallon, so no national average describes any one driver's pump price. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1930 to 2024 the per gallon price rose from $0.20 to $3.30, a multiple of 16.5 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.03% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Gasoline prices follow crude oil prices, which follow global supply, refining capacity, and taxes. Crude oil is priced in dollars worldwide, so wars, embargoes, and OPEC decisions move the pump price in every country at once, and the 1973, 1979, 2008, and 2022 shocks are all visible in this series. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.2, 'per gallon'), (1940, 0.18, 'per gallon'), (1950, 0.27, 'per gallon'), (1960, 0.31, 'per gallon'), (1970, 0.36, 'per gallon'), (1980, 1.19, 'per gallon'), (1990, 1.15, 'per gallon'), (2000, 1.51, 'per gallon'), (2010, 2.79, 'per gallon'), (2020, 2.17, 'per gallon'), (2024, 3.3, 'per gallon')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

    def run(self):

        import math

        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}

                out[key + '_last'] = p1

                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0

            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)

        outputs = compute()

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()

First-Class Postage Stamp, 1930-2024 JAH-COTY-000005 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A first-class postage stamp is the most democratic price in the record: the same stamp, the same rate, for every letter in the country, set by law and changed only by the Postal Service. This record gives the first-class rate for a one-ounce letter for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with the date each rate took effect, and converts every rate into 2024 dollars. The stamp is the database's cleanest series: no quality change, no package-size change, no regional variation, only the slow march of the rate from two cents to seventy-three cents.

History

The two-cent rate set in 1919 held through 1930, and the three-cent rate set in July 1932 held through the 1940s and 1950s, so a letter cost three cents for a quarter of a century. The rate began to move in the late 1950s: four cents in August 1958, five cents in 1963, six cents in January 1968, eight cents in May 1971, ten cents in 1974, thirteen cents in 1975, and fifteen cents in December 1978. The 1980s and 1990s brought faster rises, to twenty-five cents in 1988 and thirty-three cents in 1999, and the 2000s and 2010s carried the rate to forty-four cents in 2009 and fifty-five cents in 2019. The rate reached seventy-three cents in July 2024, after sixty-eight cents in January of that year.

Description

A first-class postage stamp is the most democratic price in the record: the same stamp, the same rate, for every letter in the country, set by law and changed only by the Postal Service. This record gives the first-class rate for a one-ounce letter for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with the date each rate took effect, and converts every rate into 2024 dollars. The stamp is the database's cleanest series: no quality change, no package-size change, no regional variation, only the slow march of the rate from two cents to seventy-three cents.

The Problem

What did a first-class stamp cost in each decade from 1930 to 2024, how fast did the rate rise, and did postage keep up with inflation or fall behind it?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the first-class rate for a one-ounce letter from the United States Postal Service rate history as documented by Wikipedia's postage-rate history and the Johnstons Archive. Step 2: record the rate in force in the named year for each decade anchor, 1930 through 2024. Step 3: convert each rate into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1930: $0.02 each; 1940: $0.03 each; 1950: $0.03 each; 1960: $0.04 each; 1970: $0.06 each; 1980: $0.15 each; 1990: $0.25 each; 2000: $0.33 each; 2010: $0.44 each; 2020: $0.55 each; 2024: $0.73 each. Gaps stated, not filled: rates changed more than once in several decades, so each figure is the rate in force in the named year, not a yearly average. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 11, "earliest_year": 1930, "latest_year": 2024, "each_first": 0.02, "each_last": 0.73, "each_multiple": 36.5, "each_cagr": 0.039}.

The Answer

A stamp cost $0.02 in 1930 and $0.73 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1930 stamp cost about $0.38, so postage became roughly twice as expensive in real terms over ninety-four years, even though the nominal rate rose more than thirty-sixfold. The real price was lowest in the 1950s, when three cents equaled about $0.39 in 2024 dollars. The series is 1930: $0.02 each; 1940: $0.03 each; 1950: $0.03 each; 1960: $0.04 each; 1970: $0.06 each; 1980: $0.15 each; 1990: $0.25 each; 2000: $0.33 each; 2010: $0.44 each; 2020: $0.55 each; 2024: $0.73 each. From 1930 to 2024 the each price rose from $0.02 to $0.73, a multiple of 36.5 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.9% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: rates changed more than once in several decades, so each figure is the rate in force in the named year, not a yearly average. The figures are the first-class rate for a one-ounce letter only; parcels, international mail, and special services have their own rates and rose faster. The 2024 figure is the July 2024 rate of seventy-three cents; the January 2024 rate was sixty-eight cents. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1930 to 2024 the each price rose from $0.02 to $0.73, a multiple of 36.5 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.9% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Postage is a regulated price, not a market price: the Postal Service sets rates to cover the cost of carrying the mail, and Congress and the Postal Regulatory Commission constrain the rises. That is why the rate held flat for decades and then jumped in steps, and why it tracks labor and transport costs more than any single commodity. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

AI

The JAH Cost of Things Librarian answers questions about this record, explains the reasoning, and runs the demo and program on request.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.02, 'each'), (1940, 0.03, 'each'), (1950, 0.03, 'each'), (1960, 0.04, 'each'), (1970, 0.06, 'each'), (1980, 0.15, 'each'), (1990, 0.25, 'each'), (2000, 0.33, 'each'), (2010, 0.44, 'each'), (2020, 0.55, 'each'), (2024, 0.73, 'each')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class StampPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1930, 0.02, 'each'), (1940, 0.03, 'each'), (1950, 0.03, 'each'), (1960, 0.04, 'each'), (1970, 0.06, 'each'), (1980, 0.15, 'each'), (1990, 0.25, 'each'), (2000, 0.33, 'each'), (2010, 0.44, 'each'), (2020, 0.55, 'each'), (2024, 0.73, 'each')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(StampPriceProgram().run())

Average Movie Ticket, 1933-2024 JAH-COTY-000006 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

The average movie ticket is the price of an evening out, and its history runs from the nickelodeon era through the multiplex and the streaming age. This record gives the average United States movie ticket price for the years the industry has published, from 1933 to 2024, with the gaps stated where the industry published nothing, and converts every published price into 2024 dollars. The ticket is the database's best lesson in real versus nominal prices: the ticket cost twenty-three cents in 1933 and $11.31 in 2024, yet in real terms it was cheapest in 1948 and most expensive in 1971.

History

The Film Daily Year Book put the average admission at twenty-three cents in 1933, in the depths of the Depression, and it stayed near a quarter through the 1930s and early 1940s. The National Association of Theatre Owners began publishing averages after the war: thirty-six cents in 1948, forty-five cents in 1954, sixty-eight cents in 1958, eighty-five cents in 1963, and $1.20 in 1967. Attendance fell as television spread, and theaters charged more per seat: $1.65 in 1971, $1.87 in 1974, and $2.69 in 1980. The multiplex era carried the average to $4.23 in 1990, $5.39 in 2000, and $7.89 in 2010. Theaters closed in the pandemic year 2020, when the estimated average was $9.18, and the estimated average reached $11.31 in 2024.

Description

The average movie ticket is the price of an evening out, and its history runs from the nickelodeon era through the multiplex and the streaming age. This record gives the average United States movie ticket price for the years the industry has published, from 1933 to 2024, with the gaps stated where the industry published nothing, and converts every published price into 2024 dollars. The ticket is the database's best lesson in real versus nominal prices: the ticket cost twenty-three cents in 1933 and $11.31 in 2024, yet in real terms it was cheapest in 1948 and most expensive in 1971.

The Problem

What did the average movie ticket cost in each published year from 1933 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was the ticket genuinely dearer in 2024 than in the 1930s once inflation is removed?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the average ticket prices from DaveManuel's compilation of Film Daily Year Book and National Association of Theatre Owners figures, cross-checked against FilmGrail. Step 2: record every published figure from 1933 to 2024, with the gaps stated where the industry published nothing. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages for the decade years, and for 1933, 1948, and 1971 use the CPI-U values 13.0, 24.1, and 40.5 from the same BLS series. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate from the first published figure (1933) to the latest (2024). The series is 1933: $0.23 each; 1940: $0.24 each; 1948: $0.36 each; 1954: $0.45 each; 1958: $0.68 each; 1963: $0.85 each; 1967: $1.20 each; 1971: $1.65 each; 1974: $1.87 each; 1980: $2.69 each; 1990: $4.23 each; 2000: $5.39 each; 2010: $7.89 each; 2020: $9.18 each; 2024: $11.31 each. Gaps stated, not filled: no industry average was published for 1950; the nearest published figures are 1948 ($0.36) and 1954 ($0.45); the industry published figures only for some years between 1948 and 1974; 2020, 2021, 2023, and 2024 are estimates from The Numbers, not NATO published averages (NATO published no average for 2020 or 2021). Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 15, "earliest_year": 1933, "latest_year": 2024, "each_first": 0.23, "each_last": 11.31, "each_multiple": 49.17, "each_cagr": 0.0437}.

The Answer

The ticket cost $0.23 in 1933 and $11.31 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1933 ticket cost about $5.55, so the real price roughly doubled over ninety-one years while the nominal price rose about forty-ninefold. The real peak was 1971, when $1.65 equaled about $12.78 in 2024 dollars, and the real low was 1948, when $0.36 equaled about $4.69 in 2024 dollars. The series is 1933: $0.23 each; 1940: $0.24 each; 1948: $0.36 each; 1954: $0.45 each; 1958: $0.68 each; 1963: $0.85 each; 1967: $1.20 each; 1971: $1.65 each; 1974: $1.87 each; 1980: $2.69 each; 1990: $4.23 each; 2000: $5.39 each; 2010: $7.89 each; 2020: $9.18 each; 2024: $11.31 each. From 1933 to 2024 the each price rose from $0.23 to $11.31, a multiple of 49.17 and a compound annual growth rate of 4.37% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: no industry average was published for 1950; the nearest published figures are 1948 ($0.36) and 1954 ($0.45); the industry published figures only for some years between 1948 and 1974; 2020, 2021, 2023, and 2024 are estimates from The Numbers, not NATO published averages (NATO published no average for 2020 or 2021). The 2020, 2021, 2023, and 2024 figures are estimates from The Numbers, not published NATO averages, because NATO published no average for 2020 or 2021 and last published one for 2022. Premium formats such as IMAX and 3D cost several dollars more than the average, and discount days and subscriptions pull the other way. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1933 to 2024 the each price rose from $0.23 to $11.31, a multiple of 49.17 and a compound annual growth rate of 4.37% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Ticket prices follow theater costs: rent, labor, and the price of the films themselves, which the studios sell as a share of the box office. The 1971 real peak reflects falling attendance after television: fewer tickets sold meant each ticket had to carry more of the theater's fixed costs. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1933, 0.23, 'each'), (1940, 0.24, 'each'), (1948, 0.36, 'each'), (1954, 0.45, 'each'), (1958, 0.68, 'each'), (1963, 0.85, 'each'), (1967, 1.2, 'each'), (1971, 1.65, 'each'), (1974, 1.87, 'each'), (1980, 2.69, 'each'), (1990, 4.23, 'each'), (2000, 5.39, 'each'), (2010, 7.89, 'each'), (2020, 9.18, 'each'), (2024, 11.31, 'each')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
