Full working program

import hashlib
class MovieTicketPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1933, 0.23, 'each'), (1940, 0.24, 'each'), (1948, 0.36, 'each'), (1954, 0.45, 'each'), (1958, 0.68, 'each'), (1963, 0.85, 'each'), (1967, 1.2, 'each'), (1971, 1.65, 'each'), (1974, 1.87, 'each'), (1980, 2.69, 'each'), (1990, 4.23, 'each'), (2000, 5.39, 'each'), (2010, 7.89, 'each'), (2020, 9.18, 'each'), (2024, 11.31, 'each')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(MovieTicketPriceProgram().run())

Average New Car, 1967-2024 JAH-COTY-000007 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

The average new car is measured here as the average expenditure on a new car: what buyers actually spent, on average, in the year, from the Bureau of Economic Analysis National Income and Product Accounts, Table 7.2.5S. This is a real dollar average, not a quality-adjusted price index, and it covers cars only, not light trucks. It must not be mixed with the Kelley Blue Book average transaction price for all new vehicles, which was about $48,000 in 2024 because it includes far more expensive trucks and SUVs. This record gives the BEA car figure for 1967, 1970, and every decade from 1980 to 2024, and converts every figure into 2024 dollars.

History

The Bureau of Economic Analysis has tracked the actual average dollar expenditure on a new car every month since 1967, and the series runs from $3,215 in 1967 to $33,332 in 2024. The price rose steadily through the 1970s and 1980s, from $3,542 in 1970 to $7,574 in 1980 and $15,045 in 1990, as cars gained equipment and safety gear. It passed $21,039 in 2000 and $24,898 in 2010, fell for two straight years in the 2008-2009 financial crisis, from $23,891 in 2007 to $23,245 in 2009, and then jumped with the pandemic shortage, rising 18.5 percent from $27,583 in 2020 to $32,680 in 2022.

Description

The average new car is measured here as the average expenditure on a new car: what buyers actually spent, on average, in the year, from the Bureau of Economic Analysis National Income and Product Accounts, Table 7.2.5S. This is a real dollar average, not a quality-adjusted price index, and it covers cars only, not light trucks. It must not be mixed with the Kelley Blue Book average transaction price for all new vehicles, which was about $48,000 in 2024 because it includes far more expensive trucks and SUVs. This record gives the BEA car figure for 1967, 1970, and every decade from 1980 to 2024, and converts every figure into 2024 dollars.

The Problem

What did the average new car cost in each decade from 1967 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and how much of the rise was cars getting better rather than cars getting dearer?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BEA average expenditure per new car from NIPA Table 7.2.5S as compiled by TrueDrivingCost, cross-checked against Cheapism. Step 2: record the 1967 starting figure, the 1970 figure, and one anchor per decade from 1980 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages, using the CPI-U value of 33.4 for 1967 from the same BLS series. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1967: $3,215 each; 1970: $3,542 each; 1980: $7,574 each; 1990: $15,045 each; 2000: $21,039 each; 2010: $24,898 each; 2020: $27,583 each; 2024: $33,332 each. Gaps stated, not filled: the BEA series starts in 1967, so there is no 1930-1960 figure in this series; a typical 1960 list price of about $2,600 is mentioned in the record text only as a labeled approximate, not as a series point. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 8, "earliest_year": 1967, "latest_year": 2024, "each_first": 3215, "each_last": 33332, "each_multiple": 10.37, "each_cagr": 0.0419}.

The Answer

The average new car cost $3,215 in 1967 and $33,332 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1967 car cost about $30,200, so the real price rose only modestly over fifty-seven years, even though the nominal price rose more than tenfold. The sharpest rise came in 2020-2022, when the average rose 18.5 percent in two years on the chip shortage and low inventories. The series is 1967: $3,215 each; 1970: $3,542 each; 1980: $7,574 each; 1990: $15,045 each; 2000: $21,039 each; 2010: $24,898 each; 2020: $27,583 each; 2024: $33,332 each. From 1967 to 2024 the each price rose from $3,215 to $33,332, a multiple of 10.37 and a compound annual growth rate of 4.19% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: the BEA series starts in 1967, so there is no 1930-1960 figure in this series; a typical 1960 list price of about $2,600 is mentioned in the record text only as a labeled approximate, not as a series point. The series covers cars only. Light trucks, pickups, vans, and SUVs cost far more: $50,167 on average in 2024, a 50.5 percent premium over the average car. A typical 1960 list price of about $2,600 is sometimes quoted, but it is a list price for a popular model, not an average expenditure, and it is not a point in this series. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

From 1967 to 2024 the each price rose from $3,215 to $33,332, a multiple of 10.37 and a compound annual growth rate of 4.19% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. The BEA figure is an average of what buyers spent, so it mixes two things: the price of a comparable car and the mix of cars bought. The mix shifted toward larger and better-equipped cars, which pushes the average up even when no single model gets dearer. The CPI new-vehicle index tries to remove quality change and rises more slowly, which is why the two measures must not be read as contradicting each other. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Sources

  • TrueDrivingCost - Average Price of a New Car Over Time, 1967-2026 (BEA Table 7.2.5S) — https://truedrivingcost.com/articles/average-new-car-price-over-time (BEA NIPA Table 7.2.5S average expenditure per new car, 1967-2026.)
  • Cheapism - Average Car Price by Year — https://www.cheapism.com/average-car-price-by-year/ (Average car price by year, cross-checking the BEA series.)

History

The Bureau of Economic Analysis has tracked the actual average dollar expenditure on a new car every month since 1967, and the series runs from $3,215 in 1967 to $33,332 in 2024. The price rose steadily through the 1970s and 1980s, from $3,542 in 1970 to $7,574 in 1980 and $15,045 in 1990, as cars gained equipment and safety gear. It passed $21,039 in 2000 and $24,898 in 2010, fell for two straight years in the 2008-2009 financial crisis, from $23,891 in 2007 to $23,245 in 2009, and then jumped with the pandemic shortage, rising 18.5 percent from $27,583 in 2020 to $32,680 in 2022.

Problem

What did the average new car cost in each decade from 1967 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and how much of the rise was cars getting better rather than cars getting dearer?

Method

Step 1: take the BEA average expenditure per new car from NIPA Table 7.2.5S as compiled by TrueDrivingCost, cross-checked against Cheapism. Step 2: record the 1967 starting figure, the 1970 figure, and one anchor per decade from 1980 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages, using the CPI-U value of 33.4 for 1967 from the same BLS series. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1967: $3,215 each; 1970: $3,542 each; 1980: $7,574 each; 1990: $15,045 each; 2000: $21,039 each; 2010: $24,898 each; 2020: $27,583 each; 2024: $33,332 each. Gaps stated, not filled: the BEA series starts in 1967, so there is no 1930-1960 figure in this series; a typical 1960 list price of about $2,600 is mentioned in the record text only as a labeled approximate, not as a series point. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars.

Dataset

BLS average retail prices and CPI-U annual averages as cited; series years 1967-2024; CPI-U 2024 annual average 313.7.

Verification path

Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Formal equation

Formal equation, in plain words

P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Results reported

The average new car cost $3,215 in 1967 and $33,332 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1967 car cost about $30,200, so the real price rose only modestly over fifty-seven years, even though the nominal price rose more than tenfold. The sharpest rise came in 2020-2022, when the average rose 18.5 percent in two years on the chip shortage and low inventories. The series is 1967: $3,215 each; 1970: $3,542 each; 1980: $7,574 each; 1990: $15,045 each; 2000: $21,039 each; 2010: $24,898 each; 2020: $27,583 each; 2024: $33,332 each. From 1967 to 2024 the each price rose from $3,215 to $33,332, a multiple of 10.37 and a compound annual growth rate of 4.19% per year.

Skeptical analysis

Gaps stated, not filled: the BEA series starts in 1967, so there is no 1930-1960 figure in this series; a typical 1960 list price of about $2,600 is mentioned in the record text only as a labeled approximate, not as a series point. The series covers cars only. Light trucks, pickups, vans, and SUVs cost far more: $50,167 on average in 2024, a 50.5 percent premium over the average car. A typical 1960 list price of about $2,600 is sometimes quoted, but it is a list price for a popular model, not an average expenditure, and it is not a point in this series. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Specification

  • dimensions: price in US dollars per each; calendar years 1967-2024; CPI-U index points
  • care: Never present a gap year as a price; never mix units across a unit change; always state the unit and the year with every figure.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One average new car in one year: what it cost and what that meant.
  • A2: The whole decade series: the price across the decades of American shopping.
  • A3: The price against the whole economy: nominal and 2024 dollars side by side.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1967, 3215, 'each'), (1970, 3542, 'each'), (1980, 7574, 'each'), (1990, 15045, 'each'), (2000, 21039, 'each'), (2010, 24898, 'each'), (2020, 27583, 'each'), (2024, 33332, 'each')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class NewCarPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1967, 3215, 'each'), (1970, 3542, 'each'), (1980, 7574, 'each'), (1990, 15045, 'each'), (2000, 21039, 'each'), (2010, 24898, 'each'), (2020, 27583, 'each'), (2024, 33332, 'each')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(NewCarPriceProgram().run())

Median New House Price, 1940-2024 JAH-COTY-000008 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

The median new house price is the middle price of all new single-family houses sold in the United States in the year, including the land, from the Census Bureau and the Department of Housing and Urban Development. This record gives that median for every decade from 1963 to 2024, and, for the earlier decades, the Census median value of all owner-occupied homes, clearly labeled as a different measure. The two measures must never be mixed into one multiple, and the demo computes multiples only within each measure.

History

The Census Bureau's new-home price series begins in 1963, when the median new house sold for $18,000, and it runs to $420,300 in 2024. Before that, the decennial census gives the median value of all owner-occupied homes: $2,938 in 1940, $7,354 in 1950, and $11,900 in 1960. The new-home median rose through every decade: $23,400 in 1970, $64,600 in 1980, $122,900 in 1990, $169,000 in 2000, $221,800 in 2010, $330,900 in 2020, and $420,300 in 2024. The fastest rises came in the 1970s, when the median nearly tripled, and after 2020, when it rose by more than a quarter in four years.

Description

The median new house price is the middle price of all new single-family houses sold in the United States in the year, including the land, from the Census Bureau and the Department of Housing and Urban Development. This record gives that median for every decade from 1963 to 2024, and, for the earlier decades, the Census median value of all owner-occupied homes, clearly labeled as a different measure. The two measures must never be mixed into one multiple, and the demo computes multiples only within each measure.

The Problem

What did the median new house cost in each decade from 1963 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and how much of the rise was general inflation rather than houses becoming genuinely dearer?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the median sales price of new houses sold from the Census Bureau's New Residential Sales annual tables, cross-checked against the Housing Almanac compilation. Step 2: record the 1963 starting figure and one anchor per decade from 1970 through 2024, and add the decennial median home values for 1940, 1950, and 1960 as a separate labeled measure. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages, using the CPI-U value of 30.6 for 1963 from the same BLS series. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate within each measure. The series is 1940: $2,938 median home value; 1950: $7,354 median home value; 1960: $11,900 median home value; 1963: $18,000 median new home price; 1970: $23,400 median new home price; 1980: $64,600 median new home price; 1990: $122,900 median new home price; 2000: $169,000 median new home price; 2010: $221,800 median new home price; 2020: $330,900 median new home price; 2024: $420,300 median new home price. Two measures appear here and must not be mixed: 1940-1960 are Census median home VALUES (all owner-occupied homes), while 1963 onward are Census median prices of NEW homes sold. The demo computes multiples only within each measure. Gaps stated, not filled: no Census median home value exists for 1930; the first housing census was 1940; the 1940-1960 figures are median values of all owner-occupied homes, a different measure from the median price of new homes sold, which starts in 1963. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 11, "earliest_year": 1940, "latest_year": 2024, "median_home_value_first": 2938, "median_home_value_last": 11900, "median_home_value_multiple": 4.05, "median_home_value_cagr": 0.0724, "median_new_home_price_first": 18000, "median_new_home_price_last": 420300, "median_new_home_price_multiple": 23.35, "median_new_home_price_cagr": 0.053}.

The Answer

The median new home sold for $18,000 in 1963 and $420,300 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1963 home cost about $184,500, so the real price more than doubled over sixty-one years while the nominal price rose more than twenty-threefold. The median home value rose from $2,938 in 1940 to $11,900 in 1960, a multiple of about 4.1 over twenty years. The series is 1940: $2,938 median home value; 1950: $7,354 median home value; 1960: $11,900 median home value; 1963: $18,000 median new home price; 1970: $23,400 median new home price; 1980: $64,600 median new home price; 1990: $122,900 median new home price; 2000: $169,000 median new home price; 2010: $221,800 median new home price; 2020: $330,900 median new home price; 2024: $420,300 median new home price. From 1940 to 1960 the median home value price rose from $2,938 to $11,900, a multiple of 4.05 and a compound annual growth rate of 7.24% per year. From 1963 to 2024 the median new home price price rose from $18,000 to $420,300, a multiple of 23.35 and a compound annual growth rate of 5.3% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: no Census median home value exists for 1930; the first housing census was 1940; the 1940-1960 figures are median values of all owner-occupied homes, a different measure from the median price of new homes sold, which starts in 1963. The median new-home price mixes regions and sizes, and it is not the price of a typical house in any one city. The median existing-home price is a different measure, $407,500 in 2024 according to the National Association of Realtors, and must not be mixed with the new-home median. The 2020 figure used here is the FRED annual value of $330,900; another compilation gives $336,900, and the difference is a revision vintage, not an error in either. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1940 to 1960 the median home value price rose from $2,938 to $11,900, a multiple of 4.05 and a compound annual growth rate of 7.24% per year. From 1963 to 2024 the median new home price price rose from $18,000 to $420,300, a multiple of 23.35 and a compound annual growth rate of 5.3% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. House prices follow land values, construction labor and materials, mortgage rates, and local supply. The post-2020 jump reflects cheap mortgages during the pandemic, then scarce supply when rates rose and owners refused to sell. Houses also got bigger: the median new home was about 1,500 square feet in 1970 and about 2,300 square feet in 2024, so part of the price rise buys more house. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

The median new home sold for $18,000 in 1963 and $420,300 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1963 home cost about $184,500, so the real price more than doubled over sixty-one years while the nominal price rose more than twenty-threefold. The median home value rose from $2,938 in 1940 to $11,900 in 1960, a multiple of about 4.1 over twenty years. The series is 1940: $2,938 median home value; 1950: $7,354 median home value; 1960: $11,900 median home value; 1963: $18,000 median new home price; 1970: $23,400 median new home price; 1980: $64,600 median new home price; 1990: $122,900 median new home price; 2000: $169,000 median new home price; 2010: $221,800 median new home price; 2020: $330,900 median new home price; 2024: $420,300 median new home price. From 1940 to 1960 the median home value price rose from $2,938 to $11,900, a multiple of 4.05 and a compound annual growth rate of 7.24% per year. From 1963 to 2024 the median new home price price rose from $18,000 to $420,300, a multiple of 23.35 and a compound annual growth rate of 5.3% per year.

Skeptical analysis

Gaps stated, not filled: no Census median home value exists for 1930; the first housing census was 1940; the 1940-1960 figures are median values of all owner-occupied homes, a different measure from the median price of new homes sold, which starts in 1963. The median new-home price mixes regions and sizes, and it is not the price of a typical house in any one city. The median existing-home price is a different measure, $407,500 in 2024 according to the National Association of Realtors, and must not be mixed with the new-home median. The 2020 figure used here is the FRED annual value of $330,900; another compilation gives $336,900, and the difference is a revision vintage, not an error in either. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Specification

  • dimensions: price in US dollars per median home value, median new home price; calendar years 1940-2024; CPI-U index points
  • care: Never present a gap year as a price; never mix units across a unit change; always state the unit and the year with every figure.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One median new house in one year: what it cost and what that meant.
  • A2: The whole decade series: the price across the decades of American shopping.
  • A3: The price against the whole economy: nominal and 2024 dollars side by side.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1940, 2938, 'median home value'), (1950, 7354, 'median home value'), (1960, 11900, 'median home value'), (1963, 18000, 'median new home price'), (1970, 23400, 'median new home price'), (1980, 64600, 'median new home price'), (1990, 122900, 'median new home price'), (2000, 169000, 'median new home price'), (2010, 221800, 'median new home price'), (2020, 330900, 'median new home price'), (2024, 420300, 'median new home price')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class NewHousePriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1940, 2938, 'median home value'), (1950, 7354, 'median home value'), (1960, 11900, 'median home value'), (1963, 18000, 'median new home price'), (1970, 23400, 'median new home price'), (1980, 64600, 'median new home price'), (1990, 122900, 'median new home price'), (2000, 169000, 'median new home price'), (2010, 221800, 'median new home price'), (2020, 330900, 'median new home price'), (2024, 420300, 'median new home price')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(NewHousePriceProgram().run())

Federal Minimum Wage, 1938-2024 JAH-COTY-000009 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

The federal minimum wage is a price too: the price of an hour of the lowest-paid labor, set by Congress rather than by the market. This record gives the federal minimum wage for every decade from its creation in 1938 to 2024, and converts every rate into 2024 dollars, so the reader can see what happened to the wage floor while everything else in this database rose. The minimum wage is the database's starkest series: it is the only one that falls in real terms for fifteen straight years.

History

The Fair Labor Standards Act set the first federal minimum wage at twenty-five cents an hour in 1938, raised it to thirty cents in October 1939, and to seventy-five cents in 1950. The rate reached one dollar in March 1956, $1.60 in February 1968, $3.10 in 1980, $3.80 in 1990, and $5.15 in 2000. The last rise, to $7.25 in July 2009, has now held for fifteen years, the longest freeze in the history of the Act. The real peak was 1968: $1.60 an hour then equaled about $14.40 in 2024 dollars, nearly twice the 2024 rate in real terms.

Description

The federal minimum wage is a price too: the price of an hour of the lowest-paid labor, set by Congress rather than by the market. This record gives the federal minimum wage for every decade from its creation in 1938 to 2024, and converts every rate into 2024 dollars, so the reader can see what happened to the wage floor while everything else in this database rose. The minimum wage is the database's starkest series: it is the only one that falls in real terms for fifteen straight years.

The Problem

What was the federal minimum wage in each decade from 1938 to 2024, how fast did it rise, and how does its real value in 2024 compare with its real value at its peak?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the federal minimum wage history from the Department of Labor Wage and Hour Division chart as documented by the Congressional Research Service and Wikipedia. Step 2: record the rate in force in the named year for 1938, 1940, and every decade from 1950 through 2024. Step 3: convert each rate into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages, using the CPI-U values of 14.1 for 1938 and 34.8 for 1968 from the same BLS series. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1938: $0.25 per hour; 1940: $0.30 per hour; 1950: $0.75 per hour; 1960: $1.00 per hour; 1970: $1.60 per hour; 1980: $3.10 per hour; 1990: $3.80 per hour; 2000: $5.15 per hour; 2010: $7.25 per hour; 2020: $7.25 per hour; 2024: $7.25 per hour. Gaps stated, not filled: no federal minimum wage existed before 1938, so there is no 1930 figure; the 1970 figure of $1.60 applied to workers covered before the 1966 amendments; workers newly covered in 1966 were at $1.45 in 1970. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 11, "earliest_year": 1938, "latest_year": 2024, "per_hour_first": 0.25, "per_hour_last": 7.25, "per_hour_multiple": 29.0, "per_hour_cagr": 0.0399}.

The Answer

The wage was $0.25 an hour in 1938 and $7.25 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1938 wage was about $5.60, so the wage floor rose only modestly in real terms over eighty-six years, and it has fallen in real terms since 2009 because the nominal rate has not moved. The real peak was 1968, when $1.60 equaled about $14.40 in 2024 dollars. The series is 1938: $0.25 per hour; 1940: $0.30 per hour; 1950: $0.75 per hour; 1960: $1.00 per hour; 1970: $1.60 per hour; 1980: $3.10 per hour; 1990: $3.80 per hour; 2000: $5.15 per hour; 2010: $7.25 per hour; 2020: $7.25 per hour; 2024: $7.25 per hour. From 1938 to 2024 the per hour price rose from $0.25 to $7.25, a multiple of 29.0 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.99% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: no federal minimum wage existed before 1938, so there is no 1930 figure; the 1970 figure of $1.60 applied to workers covered before the 1966 amendments; workers newly covered in 1966 were at $1.45 in 1970. The federal rate covers only a small share of workers now; most states and many employers pay more, so the series says nothing about what low-wage workers actually earn. The 1970 figure of $1.60 applied to workers covered before the 1966 amendments; workers newly covered in 1966 were at $1.45 in 1970. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1938 to 2024 the per hour price rose from $0.25 to $7.25, a multiple of 29.0 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.99% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. The minimum wage is set by law, so its real value falls whenever Congress leaves the nominal rate unchanged while prices rise, and it jumps only when Congress acts. State minimum wages now exceed the federal rate in most states, and many large employers pay above it, so the federal rate binds fewer workers than it once did. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1938, 0.25, 'per hour'), (1940, 0.3, 'per hour'), (1950, 0.75, 'per hour'), (1960, 1.0, 'per hour'), (1970, 1.6, 'per hour'), (1980, 3.1, 'per hour'), (1990, 3.8, 'per hour'), (2000, 5.15, 'per hour'), (2010, 7.25, 'per hour'), (2020, 7.25, 'per hour'), (2024, 7.25, 'per hour')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class MinimumWageProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1938, 0.25, 'per hour'), (1940, 0.3, 'per hour'), (1950, 0.75, 'per hour'), (1960, 1.0, 'per hour'), (1970, 1.6, 'per hour'), (1980, 3.1, 'per hour'), (1990, 3.8, 'per hour'), (2000, 5.15, 'per hour'), (2010, 7.25, 'per hour'), (2020, 7.25, 'per hour'), (2024, 7.25, 'per hour')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(MinimumWageProgram().run())

Public 4-Year College Tuition (In-State), 1970-2024 JAH-COTY-000010 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

Public four-year college tuition, in-state, is the sticker price of a year of college at a state university: tuition and required fees only, not room, board, or books. This record gives that price for the academic years ending in 1970, 1980, 1990, 2000, 2010, 2020, and 2024, and converts every figure into 2024 dollars. Tuition is the database's fastest-rising service: it roughly tripled in real terms while bread and gasoline barely moved in real terms at all.

History

In-state tuition and fees at a public four-year college cost $394 in the 1970-71 academic year, $738 in 1979-80, $1,780 in 1989-90, $3,349 in 1999-2000, $6,717 in 2009-10, $9,349 in 2019-20, and $9,872 in 2023-24. Tuition rose faster than general prices in every decade of the series: it roughly doubled in the 1980s, nearly doubled again in the 1990s, doubled again in the 2000s, and then slowed in the 2010s and 2020s as state funding stabilized and enrollment fell. The National Center for Education Statistics publishes the series in its Digest of Education Statistics, Table 330.10.

Description

Public four-year college tuition, in-state, is the sticker price of a year of college at a state university: tuition and required fees only, not room, board, or books. This record gives that price for the academic years ending in 1970, 1980, 1990, 2000, 2010, 2020, and 2024, and converts every figure into 2024 dollars. Tuition is the database's fastest-rising service: it roughly tripled in real terms while bread and gasoline barely moved in real terms at all.

The Problem

What did a year of in-state tuition at a public four-year college cost in each decade from 1970 to 2024, how fast did it rise, and how much of the rise was general inflation rather than college becoming genuinely dearer?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the average tuition and fees at public four-year institutions from NCES Digest Table 330.10, cross-checked against WalnutInvest and CollegeValuesOnline. Step 2: record the academic year ending in the named decade year, 1970 through 2024. Step 3: convert each figure into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1970: $394.00 per year; 1980: $738.00 per year; 1990: $1,780 per year; 2000: $3,349 per year; 2010: $6,717 per year; 2020: $9,349 per year; 2024: $9,872 per year. Gaps stated, not filled: the NCES series starts in 1963-64, so there is no 1930-1960 figure in this series; each figure is the academic year ending in the named decade year (1970-71, 1979-80, 1989-90, 1999-2000, 2009-10, 2019-20, 2023-24); tuition and fees only; room, board, and books are excluded. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 7, "earliest_year": 1970, "latest_year": 2024, "per_year_first": 394, "per_year_last": 9872, "per_year_multiple": 25.06, "per_year_cagr": 0.0615}.

The Answer

Tuition was $394 in 1970-71 and $9,872 in 2023-24. In 2024 dollars the 1970-71 tuition was about $3,190, so the real price roughly tripled over fifty-four years while general prices rose about eightfold in nominal terms. The fastest real rise came in the 1980s and 2000s; the slowest came after 2010. The series is 1970: $394.00 per year; 1980: $738.00 per year; 1990: $1,780 per year; 2000: $3,349 per year; 2010: $6,717 per year; 2020: $9,349 per year; 2024: $9,872 per year. From 1970 to 2024 the per year price rose from $394.00 to $9,872, a multiple of 25.06 and a compound annual growth rate of 6.15% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: the NCES series starts in 1963-64, so there is no 1930-1960 figure in this series; each figure is the academic year ending in the named decade year (1970-71, 1979-80, 1989-90, 1999-2000, 2009-10, 2019-20, 2023-24); tuition and fees only; room, board, and books are excluded. Tuition and fees only: the full cost of attendance including room and board is roughly double the figures here. Many students pay less than the sticker price after grants and scholarships, so the series describes the published price, not what the average student actually pays. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1970 to 2024 the per year price rose from $394.00 to $9,872, a multiple of 25.06 and a compound annual growth rate of 6.15% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Tuition follows state funding, which fell as a share of university budgets, and the cost of a labor-intensive service: teaching, advising, and administration cannot be automated the way a factory can, so their costs rise faster than the price of goods. Economists call this Baumol's cost disease, and it is why services like college, health care, and haircuts outpace the CPI-U while goods like bread and coffee do not. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

AI

The JAH Cost of Things Librarian answers questions about this record, explains the reasoning, and runs the demo and program on request.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1970, 394, 'per year'), (1980, 738, 'per year'), (1990, 1780, 'per year'), (2000, 3349, 'per year'), (2010, 6717, 'per year'), (2020, 9349, 'per year'), (2024, 9872, 'per year')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Cup of Coffee (Home-Brewed), 1969-2024 JAH-COTY-000011 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A cup of coffee is the cheapest luxury in the record: a few cents of ground coffee, hot water, and time. This record prices a home-brewed cup, derived from the Bureau of Labor Statistics average price of ground coffee at ten grams per cup, because no federal series prices a diner or cafe cup. The record states the derivation plainly and converts every cup price into 2024 dollars. A cafe cup costs far more, three to five dollars in 2024, because it buys labor, rent, and service, not just coffee.

History

A home-brewed cup of coffee cost 1.7 cents in 1969, 2.0 cents in 1970, and 6.5 cents in 1979, when a frost in Brazil tripled the world coffee price in two years. The cup stayed near six and a half cents through 1990, rose to 8.6 cents in 2010 and 9.8 cents in 2020, and reached 13.9 cents in 2024 as coffee prices surged again. The cup figures come from the BLS ground-coffee price at ten grams of coffee per cup, the standard home-brewed scoop, so the cup costs about one forty-fifth of a pound of ground coffee.

Description

A cup of coffee is the cheapest luxury in the record: a few cents of ground coffee, hot water, and time. This record prices a home-brewed cup, derived from the Bureau of Labor Statistics average price of ground coffee at ten grams per cup, because no federal series prices a diner or cafe cup. The record states the derivation plainly and converts every cup price into 2024 dollars. A cafe cup costs far more, three to five dollars in 2024, because it buys labor, rent, and service, not just coffee.

The Problem

What did a home-brewed cup of coffee cost in each decade from 1969 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was the cup genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1969 once inflation is removed?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average price of ground coffee per pound from DollarThen's BLS series and the DailyVitality cup series derived from it. Step 2: price the cup at ten grams of coffee, which is the price per pound times 10 divided by 453.6, and record the cup for 1969, 1970, 1979, 1980, 1990, 2000, 2010, 2020, and 2024. Step 3: convert each cup price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages, using the CPI-U value of 36.7 for 1969 from the same BLS series. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1969: $0.02 per cup; 1970: $0.02 per cup; 1979: $0.07 per cup; 1980: $0.07 per cup; 1990: $0.07 per cup; 2000: $0.08 per cup; 2010: $0.09 per cup; 2020: $0.10 per cup; 2024: $0.14 per cup. Gaps stated, not filled: no federal diner or cafe cup series exists, so this record prices a home-brewed cup derived from the BLS ground-coffee price at 10 grams per cup; the 1980 and 2000 cup figures are derived arithmetically from the BLS ground-coffee prices of $3.14 and $3.45 per pound at 10 grams per cup, not separately published cup prices; no cup figure exists for 1960. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 9, "earliest_year": 1969, "latest_year": 2024, "per_cup_first": 0.017, "per_cup_last": 0.139, "per_cup_multiple": 8.18, "per_cup_cagr": 0.0389}.

The Answer

A home-brewed cup cost 1.7 cents in 1969 and 13.9 cents in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1969 cup cost about 15 cents, so the real price of a home-brewed cup was slightly lower in 2024 than in 1969, even though the nominal price rose more than eightfold. The cup was dearest in real terms in 1979, after the Brazilian frost. The series is 1969: $0.02 per cup; 1970: $0.02 per cup; 1979: $0.07 per cup; 1980: $0.07 per cup; 1990: $0.07 per cup; 2000: $0.08 per cup; 2010: $0.09 per cup; 2020: $0.10 per cup; 2024: $0.14 per cup. From 1969 to 2024 the per cup price rose from $0.02 to $0.14, a multiple of 8.18 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.89% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: no federal diner or cafe cup series exists, so this record prices a home-brewed cup derived from the BLS ground-coffee price at 10 grams per cup; the 1980 and 2000 cup figures are derived arithmetically from the BLS ground-coffee prices of $3.14 and $3.45 per pound at 10 grams per cup, not separately published cup prices; no cup figure exists for 1960. A cafe cup costs far more than the figures here because it buys labor, rent, and service, not just coffee. The 1980 and 2000 cup figures are derived arithmetically from the ground-coffee price, not separately published, and the ten-gram scoop is a standard home measure, not a universal one. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1969 to 2024 the per cup price rose from $0.02 to $0.14, a multiple of 8.18 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.89% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Coffee prices follow the world bean market: frost in Brazil, drought in Vietnam, and the dollar's exchange rate move the price, and the 1970s spike and the 2022-2024 surge are both visible in this series. The long real decline of the home-brewed cup reflects higher coffee yields and cheaper shipping, which held the bean price down while everything else rose. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

History

A home-brewed cup of coffee cost 1.7 cents in 1969, 2.0 cents in 1970, and 6.5 cents in 1979, when a frost in Brazil tripled the world coffee price in two years. The cup stayed near six and a half cents through 1990, rose to 8.6 cents in 2010 and 9.8 cents in 2020, and reached 13.9 cents in 2024 as coffee prices surged again. The cup figures come from the BLS ground-coffee price at ten grams of coffee per cup, the standard home-brewed scoop, so the cup costs about one forty-fifth of a pound of ground coffee.

Problem

What did a home-brewed cup of coffee cost in each decade from 1969 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was the cup genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1969 once inflation is removed?

Method

Step 1: take the BLS average price of ground coffee per pound from DollarThen's BLS series and the DailyVitality cup series derived from it. Step 2: price the cup at ten grams of coffee, which is the price per pound times 10 divided by 453.6, and record the cup for 1969, 1970, 1979, 1980, 1990, 2000, 2010, 2020, and 2024. Step 3: convert each cup price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages, using the CPI-U value of 36.7 for 1969 from the same BLS series. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1969: $0.02 per cup; 1970: $0.02 per cup; 1979: $0.07 per cup; 1980: $0.07 per cup; 1990: $0.07 per cup; 2000: $0.08 per cup; 2010: $0.09 per cup; 2020: $0.10 per cup; 2024: $0.14 per cup. Gaps stated, not filled: no federal diner or cafe cup series exists, so this record prices a home-brewed cup derived from the BLS ground-coffee price at 10 grams per cup; the 1980 and 2000 cup figures are derived arithmetically from the BLS ground-coffee prices of $3.14 and $3.45 per pound at 10 grams per cup, not separately published cup prices; no cup figure exists for 1960. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars.

Dataset

BLS average retail prices and CPI-U annual averages as cited; series years 1969-2024; CPI-U 2024 annual average 313.7.

A cafe cup costs far more than the figures here because it buys labor, rent, and service, not just coffee. The 1980 and 2000 cup figures are derived arithmetically from the ground-coffee price, not separately published, and the ten-gram scoop is a standard home measure, not a universal one. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1969, 0.017, 'per cup'), (1970, 0.02, 'per cup'), (1979, 0.065, 'per cup'), (1980, 0.069, 'per cup'), (1990, 0.065, 'per cup'), (2000, 0.076, 'per cup'), (2010, 0.086, 'per cup'), (2020, 0.098, 'per cup'), (2024, 0.139, 'per cup')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class CoffeeCupPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1969, 0.017, 'per cup'), (1970, 0.02, 'per cup'), (1979, 0.065, 'per cup'), (1980, 0.069, 'per cup'), (1990, 0.065, 'per cup'), (2000, 0.076, 'per cup'), (2010, 0.086, 'per cup'), (2020, 0.098, 'per cup'), (2024, 0.139, 'per cup')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(CoffeeCupPriceProgram().run())

Pound of Ground Coffee, 1950-2024 JAH-COTY-000012 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A pound of ground coffee is the raw material of the American morning, and its price is set in world markets rather than in American stores. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of 100 percent ground roast coffee per pound for 1950, 1969, 1970, 1979, and every decade from 1980 to 2024, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Coffee is the database's clearest case of a commodity price: it jumps with frost and drought, falls with good harvests, and drifts down in real terms over the long run.

History

Ground coffee cost 79.4 cents a pound in 1950, according to BLS Bulletin 1055, and it was still only 77 cents in 1969. A frost in Brazil in the 1970s tripled the price: $0.91 in 1970, $2.97 in 1979, and $3.14 in 1980. The price then fell back and stayed near three dollars through the 1990s, at $2.97 in 1990, and rose slowly to $3.45 in 2000, $3.91 in 2010, and $4.43 in 2020. The 2020s brought the sharpest rise in the series: $5.89 in 2022, $6.16 in 2023, and $6.32 in 2024, with the 2025 average reaching $8.18.

Description

A pound of ground coffee is the raw material of the American morning, and its price is set in world markets rather than in American stores. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of 100 percent ground roast coffee per pound for 1950, 1969, 1970, 1979, and every decade from 1980 to 2024, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Coffee is the database's clearest case of a commodity price: it jumps with frost and drought, falls with good harvests, and drifts down in real terms over the long run.

The Problem

What did a pound of ground coffee cost in each decade from 1950 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was coffee genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1950 once inflation is removed?

The Worked Solution

Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1950: $0.79 per lb; 1969: $0.77 per lb; 1970: $0.91 per lb; 1979: $2.97 per lb; 1980: $3.14 per lb; 1990: $2.97 per lb; 2000: $3.45 per lb; 2010: $3.91 per lb; 2020: $4.43 per lb; 2024: $6.32 per lb. The Answer

Ground coffee cost $0.79 a pound in 1950 and $6.32 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1950 pound cost about $10.28, so coffee became genuinely cheaper in real terms over seventy-four years, even after the 2022-2024 surge. The real peak was 1980, when $3.14 equaled about $11.95 in 2024 dollars. The series is 1950: $0.79 per lb; 1969: $0.77 per lb; 1970: $0.91 per lb; 1979: $2.97 per lb; 1980: $3.14 per lb; 1990: $2.97 per lb; 2000: $3.45 per lb; 2010: $3.91 per lb; 2020: $4.43 per lb; 2024: $6.32 per lb. From 1950 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.79 to $6.32, a multiple of 8.0 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.85% per year.
