The 2020s brought the sharpest rise in the series: $5.89 in 2022, $6.16 in 2023, and $6.32 in 2024, with the 2025 average reaching $8.18.

Problem

What did a pound of ground coffee cost in each decade from 1950 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was coffee genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1950 once inflation is removed?

Method

Step 1: take the BLS average price of ground roast coffee per pound from DollarThen's BLS series APU0000717311, with the 1950 figure from BLS Bulletin 1055. Step 2: record the 1950, 1969, 1970, and 1979 figures and one anchor per decade from 1980 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1950: $0.79 per lb; 1969: $0.77 per lb; 1970: $0.91 per lb; 1979: $2.97 per lb; 1980: $3.14 per lb; 1990: $2.97 per lb; 2000: $3.45 per lb; 2010: $3.91 per lb; 2020: $4.43 per lb; 2024: $6.32 per lb. Gaps stated, not filled: no ground-coffee figure exists for 1960 in the sources consulted; BLS published no ground-coffee average price in 2018 or 2019. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars.

Dataset

BLS average retail prices and CPI-U annual averages as cited; series years 1950-2024; CPI-U 2024 annual average 313.7.

Verification path

Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Formal equation

P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1

Formal equation, in plain words

P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

Ground coffee cost $0.79 a pound in 1950 and $6.32 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1950 pound cost about $10.28, so coffee became genuinely cheaper in real terms over seventy-four years, even after the 2022-2024 surge. The real peak was 1980, when $3.14 equaled about $11.95 in 2024 dollars. The series is 1950: $0.79 per lb; 1969: $0.77 per lb; 1970: $0.91 per lb; 1979: $2.97 per lb; 1980: $3.14 per lb; 1990: $2.97 per lb; 2000: $3.45 per lb; 2010: $3.91 per lb; 2020: $4.43 per lb; 2024: $6.32 per lb. From 1950 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.79 to $6.32, a multiple of 8.0 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.85% per year.

Skeptical analysis

Gaps stated, not filled: no ground-coffee figure exists for 1960 in the sources consulted; BLS published no ground-coffee average price in 2018 or 2019. The BLS pound is 100 percent ground roast, all sizes; specialty and single-origin coffees cost far more and are not in this series. BLS published no ground-coffee average price in 2018 or 2019, and the 2025 average of $8.18 sits outside this record's span. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Specification

  • dimensions: price in US dollars per per lb; calendar years 1950-2024; CPI-U index points
  • care: Never present a gap year as a price; never mix units across a unit change; always state the unit and the year with every figure.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One pound of ground coffee in one year: what it cost and what that meant.
  • A2: The whole decade series: the price across the decades of American shopping.
  • A3: The price against the whole economy: nominal and 2024 dollars side by side.

Key figures

  • points: 10
  • earliest_year: 1950
  • latest_year: 2024
  • per_lb_first: 0.79
  • per_lb_last: 6.32
  • per_lb_multiple: 8.0
  • per_lb_cagr: 0.0285

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1950, 0.79, 'per lb'), (1969, 0.77, 'per lb'), (1970, 0.91, 'per lb'), (1979, 2.97, 'per lb'), (1980, 3.14, 'per lb'), (1990, 2.97, 'per lb'), (2000, 3.45, 'per lb'), (2010, 3.91, 'per lb'), (2020, 4.43, 'per lb'), (2024, 6.32, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class GroundCoffeePriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1950, 0.79, 'per lb'), (1969, 0.77, 'per lb'), (1970, 0.91, 'per lb'), (1979, 2.97, 'per lb'), (1980, 3.14, 'per lb'), (1990, 2.97, 'per lb'), (2000, 3.45, 'per lb'), (2010, 3.91, 'per lb'), (2020, 4.43, 'per lb'), (2024, 6.32, 'per lb')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(GroundCoffeePriceProgram().run())

Potatoes, 1930-2024 JAH-COTY-000013 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

Potatoes are the humble staple of the American price record: the Bureau of Labor Statistics has priced them since the early retail-price program, first by the ten-pound bag and later by the pound. This record gives the average retail price of potatoes for every decade from 1930 to 2024, keeps the two units strictly apart, and converts every price into 2024 dollars within its own unit. Potatoes replaced the men's suit in this database, because no federal average-price series and no reliable decade-by-decade national series exists for a suit, while potatoes carry a full BLS series; the swap is recorded in the wave report.

History

Potatoes were sold by the ten-pound bag for the first forty years of the series: thirty-six cents in 1930, twenty-four cents in the Depression year 1940, forty-six cents in 1950, seventy-two cents in 1960, and ninety cents in 1970. The published series switches to the pound in 1980, at nineteen cents, and the pound rose to thirty-seven cents in 1990, thirty-eight cents in 2000, fifty-eight cents in 2010, eighty-one cents in 2020, and ninety-eight cents in 2024. Potatoes are the cheapest source of calories in the record, and their price tracks the harvest more than any other staple.

Description

Potatoes are the humble staple of the American price record: the Bureau of Labor Statistics has priced them since the early retail-price program, first by the ten-pound bag and later by the pound. This record gives the average retail price of potatoes for every decade from 1930 to 2024, keeps the two units strictly apart, and converts every price into 2024 dollars within its own unit. Potatoes replaced the men's suit in this database, because no federal average-price series and no reliable decade-by-decade national series exists for a suit, while potatoes carry a full BLS series; the swap is recorded in the wave report.

The Problem

What did potatoes cost in each decade from 1930 to 2024, and how do the ten-pound decades compare with the per-pound decades without mixing the two units?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average retail price of potatoes from the Stacker compilation of BLS average prices. Step 2: record one anchor per decade, keeping the published unit for each year: per ten pounds for 1930 through 1970, per pound for 1980 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars within its own unit using the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate separately for the ten-pound span (1930-1970) and the per-pound span (1980-2024), and never compute a multiple across the unit change. The series is 1930: $0.36 per 10 lb; 1940: $0.24 per 10 lb; 1950: $0.46 per 10 lb; 1960: $0.72 per 10 lb; 1970: $0.90 per 10 lb; 1980: $0.19 per lb; 1990: $0.37 per lb; 2000: $0.38 per lb; 2010: $0.58 per lb; 2020: $0.81 per lb; 2024: $0.98 per lb. The unit changes in the published series: 1930 through 1970 are per 10 pounds, and 1980 onward is per pound, so the demo computes multiples only within each unit, never across the change. Gaps stated, not filled: BLS published no average retail food prices from July 1978 to December 1979 during the CPI revision. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 11, "earliest_year": 1930, "latest_year": 2024, "per_10_lb_first": 0.36, "per_10_lb_last": 0.9, "per_10_lb_multiple": 2.5, "per_10_lb_cagr": 0.0232, "per_lb_first": 0.19, "per_lb_last": 0.98, "per_lb_multiple": 5.16, "per_lb_cagr": 0.038}.

The Answer

The ten-pound bag cost $0.36 in 1930 and $0.90 in 1970. The pound cost $0.19 in 1980 and $0.98 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1930 ten-pound bag cost about $6.76, or about $0.68 a pound, so potatoes were cheaper per pound in 1930 than in 2024 in real terms. The pound rose from about $0.72 in 2024 dollars in 1980 to $0.98 in 2024, so potatoes became genuinely dearer in real terms after 1980. The series is 1930: $0.36 per 10 lb; 1940: $0.24 per 10 lb; 1950: $0.46 per 10 lb; 1960: $0.72 per 10 lb; 1970: $0.90 per 10 lb; 1980: $0.19 per lb; 1990: $0.37 per lb; 2000: $0.38 per lb; 2010: $0.58 per lb; 2020: $0.81 per lb; 2024: $0.98 per lb. From 1930 to 1970 the per 10 lb price rose from $0.36 to $0.90, a multiple of 2.5 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.32% per year. From 1980 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.19 to $0.98, a multiple of 5.16 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.8% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: BLS published no average retail food prices from July 1978 to December 1979 during the CPI revision. The BLS pound is white potatoes, fresh; the ten-pound figures are a different pack size, and bulk bags cost less per pound than loose potatoes. The series says nothing about processed potatoes, which carry most of the potato market today. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1930 to 1970 the per 10 lb price rose from $0.36 to $0.90, a multiple of 2.5 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.32% per year. From 1980 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.19 to $0.98, a multiple of 5.16 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.8% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Potato prices follow the harvest: weather, blight, and acreage move the price year to year, and the fresh market is thin, so a bad harvest shows up at the store within weeks. The real rise after 1980 reflects the shift toward fresh table potatoes and higher transport and storage costs. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.36, 'per 10 lb'), (1940, 0.24, 'per 10 lb'), (1950, 0.46, 'per 10 lb'), (1960, 0.72, 'per 10 lb'), (1970, 0.9, 'per 10 lb'), (1980, 0.19, 'per lb'), (1990, 0.37, 'per lb'), (2000, 0.38, 'per lb'), (2010, 0.58, 'per lb'), (2020, 0.81, 'per lb'), (2024, 0.98, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class PotatoPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1930, 0.36, 'per 10 lb'), (1940, 0.24, 'per 10 lb'), (1950, 0.46, 'per 10 lb'), (1960, 0.72, 'per 10 lb'), (1970, 0.9, 'per 10 lb'), (1980, 0.19, 'per lb'), (1990, 0.37, 'per lb'), (2000, 0.38, 'per lb'), (2010, 0.58, 'per lb'), (2020, 0.81, 'per lb'), (2024, 0.98, 'per lb')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(PotatoPriceProgram().run())

Pound of White Flour, 1950-2024 JAH-COTY-000014 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A pound of white all-purpose flour is the cheapest ingredient in the American kitchen, and its price is the purest read on the wheat market in this database. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of white flour per pound for 1950, 1970, and every decade from 1980 to 2024, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Flour is the database's best example of a price that falls in real terms for seventy years: it cost about $1.28 a pound in 2024 dollars in 1950 and $0.55 in 2024.

History

White flour cost 49.1 cents for five pounds in 1950, about ten cents a pound, and twelve cents a pound in 1970. The BLS average-price series begins in 1980, when flour averaged twenty-one cents a pound, and the pound rose to twenty-five cents in 1990, twenty-nine cents in 2000, and forty-eight cents in 2010, after the global wheat shortage of 2008 pushed the price to fifty-one cents. The pound fell back to forty-four cents in 2020 and rose to fifty-five cents in 2024, near the record monthly high of fifty-seven cents in June 2024. Flour replaced the pair of men's leather shoes in this database, because no federal average-price series and no reliable decade-by-decade national series exists for shoes, while flour carries a full BLS series; the swap is recorded in the wave report.

Description

A pound of white all-purpose flour is the cheapest ingredient in the American kitchen, and its price is the purest read on the wheat market in this database. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of white flour per pound for 1950, 1970, and every decade from 1980 to 2024, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Flour is the database's best example of a price that falls in real terms for seventy years: it cost about $1.28 a pound in 2024 dollars in 1950 and $0.55 in 2024.

The Problem

What did a pound of white flour cost in each decade from 1950 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was flour genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1950 once inflation is removed?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average price of white all-purpose flour per pound from the USA Cost of Living monthly table (BLS series APU0000701111), computing the 1990, 2000, and 2010 annual averages as the mean of the twelve published monthly values, and cross-check against GOBankingRates. Step 2: add the 1950 figure from BLS Bulletin 1055 (49.1 cents per five pounds) and the 1970 figure from the CheddarDen household-price compilation. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1950: $0.10 per lb; 1970: $0.12 per lb; 1980: $0.21 per lb; 1990: $0.25 per lb; 2000: $0.29 per lb; 2010: $0.48 per lb; 2020: $0.44 per lb; 2024: $0.55 per lb. Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS flour series starts in 1980; the 1950 figure comes from BLS Bulletin 1055 and the 1970 figure from a household-price compilation; no flour figure exists for 1930, 1940, or 1960 in the sources consulted. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 8, "earliest_year": 1950, "latest_year": 2024, "per_lb_first": 0.098, "per_lb_last": 0.55, "per_lb_multiple": 5.61, "per_lb_cagr": 0.0236}.

The Answer

Flour cost about $0.10 a pound in 1950 and $0.55 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1950 pound cost about $1.28, so flour became much cheaper in real terms over seventy-four years, even after the 2008 and 2022 wheat shocks. The 2010 annual average of $0.48 is the mean of the twelve published BLS monthly values; GOBankingRates gives $0.49 for the same year, and the difference is rounding and vintage. The series is 1950: $0.10 per lb; 1970: $0.12 per lb; 1980: $0.21 per lb; 1990: $0.25 per lb; 2000: $0.29 per lb; 2010: $0.48 per lb; 2020: $0.44 per lb; 2024: $0.55 per lb. From 1950 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.10 to $0.55, a multiple of 5.61 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.36% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS flour series starts in 1980; the 1950 figure comes from BLS Bulletin 1055 and the 1970 figure from a household-price compilation; no flour figure exists for 1930, 1940, or 1960 in the sources consulted. The BLS pound is white all-purpose flour, all package sizes; self-rising, organic, and specialty flours cost more. The 2010 figure is the mean of rounded monthly values, so it carries about a cent of rounding uncertainty, and the 1950 and 1970 figures come from older and compilation sources rather than the modern BLS series. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1950 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.10 to $0.55, a multiple of 5.61 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.36% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Flour prices follow wheat prices with a short lag, and wheat prices follow world harvests, export policy, and the dollar. The 2008 spike followed the global wheat shortage, and the 2022 rise followed the invasion of Ukraine, which cut Black Sea wheat exports. Milling and transport add only a few cents a pound, so the flour price is nearly a pure wheat price. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

AI

The JAH Cost of Things Librarian answers questions about this record, explains the reasoning, and runs the demo and program on request.

Sources

  • GOBankingRates - How Much These 12 Grocery Staples Cost Every Year Since 1980 (BLS) — https://www.gobankingrates.com/money/economy/heres-how-much-these-grocery-staples-cost-yearly/ (Grocery staples by year since 1980, cross-checking the BLS flour figures.)
  • BLS Bulletin 1055 (1952) - Retail Prices of Food, 1950 (mirror) — https://vitrine.hraedon.com/archive/sources/bls-bulletin-1055-1952.html (BLS Bulletin 1055: white flour 49.1 cents per 5 pounds in 1950.)

History

Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS flour series starts in 1980; the 1950 figure comes from BLS Bulletin 1055 and the 1970 figure from a household-price compilation; no flour figure exists for 1930, 1940, or 1960 in the sources consulted. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1950, 0.098, 'per lb'), (1970, 0.12, 'per lb'), (1980, 0.21, 'per lb'), (1990, 0.25, 'per lb'), (2000, 0.29, 'per lb'), (2010, 0.48, 'per lb'), (2020, 0.44, 'per lb'), (2024, 0.55, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Average Monthly Rent, 1940-2024 JAH-COTY-000015 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

Average monthly rent is the largest bill in most household budgets, and the Census median gross rent is the longest national series for it. This record gives the Census median gross rent for every decade from 1940 to 2024, and converts every figure into 2024 dollars. Rent is the database's second-fastest-rising service after college tuition: it more than doubled in real terms while bread and gasoline barely moved in real terms at all.

History

The Census Bureau first counted rents in 1940, when the median gross rent was twenty-seven dollars a month. It rose to forty-two dollars in 1950, seventy-one dollars in 1960, and one hundred eight dollars in 1970, then more than doubled in the inflationary 1970s to two hundred forty-three dollars in 1980. The median reached four hundred forty-seven dollars in 1990, six hundred two dollars in 2000, eight hundred fifty-five dollars in 2010, one thousand ninety-six dollars in 2020, and one thousand four hundred six dollars in 2024. Gross rent means contract rent plus utilities, so it is the closest the Census comes to what a renter actually pays each month.

Description

Average monthly rent is the largest bill in most household budgets, and the Census median gross rent is the longest national series for it. This record gives the Census median gross rent for every decade from 1940 to 2024, and converts every figure into 2024 dollars. Rent is the database's second-fastest-rising service after college tuition: it more than doubled in real terms while bread and gasoline barely moved in real terms at all.

The Problem

What was the average monthly rent in each decade from 1940 to 2024, how fast did it rise, and how much of the rise was general inflation rather than housing becoming genuinely dearer?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the Census Bureau median gross rent from the decennial census tables, cross-checked against the Average-Rent and iPropertyManagement compilations. Step 2: record one anchor per decade, 1940 through 2024. Step 3: convert each rent into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1940: $27.00 per month; 1950: $42.00 per month; 1960: $71.00 per month; 1970: $108.00 per month; 1980: $243.00 per month; 1990: $447.00 per month; 2000: $602.00 per month; 2010: $855.00 per month; 2020: $1,096 per month; 2024: $1,406 per month. Gaps stated, not filled: no Census median gross rent exists for 1930; the first housing census was 1940; the 2024 figure is $1,406 from the Average-Rent compilation of American Community Survey data; a separate ACS 2024 1-year estimate gives $1,487, and the difference is a survey vintage, not an error in either. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 10, "earliest_year": 1940, "latest_year": 2024, "per_month_first": 27, "per_month_last": 1406, "per_month_multiple": 52.07, "per_month_cagr": 0.0482}.

The Answer

Rent was $27 a month in 1940 and $1,406 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1940 rent was about $605, so rent more than doubled in real terms over eighty-four years. The 1970 rent of $108 equaled about $873 in 2024 dollars, so the 2024 rent is about 60 percent higher in real terms than the 1970 rent. The series is 1940: $27.00 per month; 1950: $42.00 per month; 1960: $71.00 per month; 1970: $108.00 per month; 1980: $243.00 per month; 1990: $447.00 per month; 2000: $602.00 per month; 2010: $855.00 per month; 2020: $1,096 per month; 2024: $1,406 per month. From 1940 to 2024 the per month price rose from $27.00 to $1,406, a multiple of 52.07 and a compound annual growth rate of 4.82% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: no Census median gross rent exists for 1930; the first housing census was 1940; the 2024 figure is $1,406 from the Average-Rent compilation of American Community Survey data; a separate ACS 2024 1-year estimate gives $1,487, and the difference is a survey vintage, not an error in either. Median gross rent mixes apartments of all sizes and ages, and it is not the rent of a comparable unit; units got bigger and better equipped over the century, so part of the rise buys a better home. The 2024 figure is a compilation of American Community Survey data, and a separate ACS 2024 1-year estimate gives $1,487. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1940 to 2024 the per month price rose from $27.00 to $1,406, a multiple of 52.07 and a compound annual growth rate of 4.82% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Rents follow incomes, local supply, and mortgage rates: when houses are scarce and mortgages are dear, more people rent and rents rise. Rent control held some cities down for decades, and the post-2020 rise reflects scarce supply after the pandemic building slowdown. Housing is a service that cannot be shipped from a cheaper place, so its price rises with local wages. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

AI

The JAH Cost of Things Librarian answers questions about this record, explains the reasoning, and runs the demo and program on request.

  • Average-Rent - Rent Trends — https://average-rent.com/rent-trends/ (Rent trends, including the 2024 median gross rent of $1,406.)
  • iPropertyManagement - Average Rent by Year — https://ipropertymanagement.com/research/average-rent-by-year (Average rent by year, cross-checking the Census rent series.)

Specification

  • dimensions: price in US dollars per per month; calendar years 1940-2024; CPI-U index points
  • care: Never present a gap year as a price; never mix units across a unit change; always state the unit and the year with every figure.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One average monthly rent in one year: what it cost and what that meant.
  • A2: The whole decade series: the price across the decades of American shopping.
  • A3: The price against the whole economy: nominal and 2024 dollars side by side.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1940, 27, 'per month'), (1950, 42, 'per month'), (1960, 71, 'per month'), (1970, 108, 'per month'), (1980, 243, 'per month'), (1990, 447, 'per month'), (2000, 602, 'per month'), (2010, 855, 'per month'), (2020, 1096, 'per month'), (2024, 1406, 'per month')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class RentPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1940, 27, 'per month'), (1950, 42, 'per month'), (1960, 71, 'per month'), (1970, 108, 'per month'), (1980, 243, 'per month'), (1990, 447, 'per month'), (2000, 602, 'per month'), (2010, 855, 'per month'), (2020, 1096, 'per month'), (2024, 1406, 'per month')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(RentPriceProgram().run())

Pound of Ground Beef, 1984-2024 JAH-COTY-000016 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A pound of 100 percent ground beef is the most-bought meat in the American record, and its price series is the shortest in this database, starting only in 1984. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of ground beef per pound for 1984, 1990, 2000, 2010, 2020, and 2024, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Beef is the database's clearest case of a price driven by the size of the herd: when ranchers keep fewer cattle, the price rises, and rebuilding a herd takes years.

History

The Bureau of Labor Statistics began publishing an average price for 100 percent ground beef in 1984, when it cost $1.29 a pound. The price rose to $1.59 in 1990, slipped back to $1.57 in 2000, and then climbed: $2.37 in 2010, $4.12 in 2020, and $5.39 in 2024. The sharpest moves came in 2014 and 2015, when drought cut the cattle herd and the price jumped to $3.90 and then $4.17, and again in 2020-2024, when the herd shrank to its smallest in decades and the price rose by nearly a third in four years.

Description

A pound of 100 percent ground beef is the most-bought meat in the American record, and its price series is the shortest in this database, starting only in 1984. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of ground beef per pound for 1984, 1990, 2000, 2010, 2020, and 2024, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Beef is the database's clearest case of a price driven by the size of the herd: when ranchers keep fewer cattle, the price rises, and rebuilding a herd takes years.

The Problem

What did a pound of ground beef cost in each decade from 1984 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was beef genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1984 once inflation is removed?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average price of 100 percent ground beef per pound from DollarThen's BLS series APU0000703112. Step 2: record the 1984 starting figure and one anchor per decade from 1990 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages, using the CPI-U value of 103.9 for 1984 from the same BLS series. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1984: $1.29 per lb; 1990: $1.59 per lb; 2000: $1.57 per lb; 2010: $2.37 per lb; 2020: $4.12 per lb; 2024: $5.39 per lb. Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS ground-beef series starts in 1984, so there is no 1930-1980 figure in this series; ground chuck, a different item, averaged $1.80 per pound in 1980 and is mentioned only as a labeled cross-check, not as a series point. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 6, "earliest_year": 1984, "latest_year": 2024, "per_lb_first": 1.29, "per_lb_last": 5.39, "per_lb_multiple": 4.18, "per_lb_cagr": 0.0364}.

The Answer

Ground beef cost $1.29 a pound in 1984 and $5.39 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1984 pound cost about $3.89, so beef became genuinely dearer in real terms over forty years, rising from about $3.89 to $5.39. The price was flat in real terms through 2000 and then rose steadily as the cattle herd shrank. The series is 1984: $1.29 per lb; 1990: $1.59 per lb; 2000: $1.57 per lb; 2010: $2.37 per lb; 2020: $4.12 per lb; 2024: $5.39 per lb. From 1984 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $1.29 to $5.39, a multiple of 4.18 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.64% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS ground-beef series starts in 1984, so there is no 1930-1980 figure in this series; ground chuck, a different item, averaged $1.80 per pound in 1980 and is mentioned only as a labeled cross-check, not as a series point. The series covers 100 percent ground beef only; ground chuck and lean blends are different items with different prices, and the 1980 ground-chuck figure of $1.80 is not part of this series. The series starts in 1984, so it cannot show the beef prices of the 1970s, when the herd was far larger. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1984 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $1.29 to $5.39, a multiple of 4.18 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.64% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Beef prices follow the cattle cycle: ranchers expand the herd when calf prices are high and cull it when feed is dear, and the cycle runs about ten years. Drought in the cattle states forces early sales, which first floods the market and then leaves it short, so the price falls and then rises. Feed corn and soybeans set the floor under the price. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Sources

  • DollarThen - Ground Beef Price History 1984-2026 (BLS APU0000703112) — https://www.dollarthen.com/prices/ground-beef/ (BLS ground-beef annual averages (APU0000703112), 1984-2026.)
  • in2013dollars - Uncooked Ground Beef Price Inflation — https://www.in2013dollars.com/Uncooked-ground-beef/price-inflation (Uncooked ground beef price inflation, cross-checking the BLS series.)
  • MealFan - Grocery Price Index 2026 (BLS Average Price Data) — https://www.mealfan.com/grocery-price-index/ (BLS average price data for ground beef, January 2020 and October 2026 points.)

History

The Bureau of Labor Statistics began publishing an average price for 100 percent ground beef in 1984, when it cost $1.29 a pound. The price rose to $1.59 in 1990, slipped back to $1.57 in 2000, and then climbed: $2.37 in 2010, $4.12 in 2020, and $5.39 in 2024. The sharpest moves came in 2014 and 2015, when drought cut the cattle herd and the price jumped to $3.90 and then $4.17, and again in 2020-2024, when the herd shrank to its smallest in decades and the price rose by nearly a third in four years.

Problem

What did a pound of ground beef cost in each decade from 1984 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was beef genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1984 once inflation is removed?

Method

Step 1: take the BLS average price of 100 percent ground beef per pound from DollarThen's BLS series APU0000703112. Step 2: record the 1984 starting figure and one anchor per decade from 1990 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages, using the CPI-U value of 103.9 for 1984 from the same BLS series. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1984: $1.29 per lb; 1990: $1.59 per lb; 2000: $1.57 per lb; 2010: $2.37 per lb; 2020: $4.12 per lb; 2024: $5.39 per lb. Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS ground-beef series starts in 1984, so there is no 1930-1980 figure in this series; ground chuck, a different item, averaged $1.80 per pound in 1980 and is mentioned only as a labeled cross-check, not as a series point. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars.

Dataset

BLS average retail prices and CPI-U annual averages as cited; series years 1984-2024; CPI-U 2024 annual average 313.7.

Verification path

Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Formal equation

P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1

Formal equation, in plain words

P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

Ground beef cost $1.29 a pound in 1984 and $5.39 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1984 pound cost about $3.89, so beef became genuinely dearer in real terms over forty years, rising from about $3.89 to $5.39. The price was flat in real terms through 2000 and then rose steadily as the cattle herd shrank. The series is 1984: $1.29 per lb; 1990: $1.59 per lb; 2000: $1.57 per lb; 2010: $2.37 per lb; 2020: $4.12 per lb; 2024: $5.39 per lb. From 1984 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $1.29 to $5.39, a multiple of 4.18 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.64% per year.

Skeptical analysis

Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS ground-beef series starts in 1984, so there is no 1930-1980 figure in this series; ground chuck, a different item, averaged $1.80 per pound in 1980 and is mentioned only as a labeled cross-check, not as a series point. The series covers 100 percent ground beef only; ground chuck and lean blends are different items with different prices, and the 1980 ground-chuck figure of $1.80 is not part of this series. The series starts in 1984, so it cannot show the beef prices of the 1970s, when the herd was far larger. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Specification

  • dimensions: price in US dollars per per lb; calendar years 1984-2024; CPI-U index points
  • care: Never present a gap year as a price; never mix units across a unit change; always state the unit and the year with every figure.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One pound of ground beef in one year: what it cost and what that meant.
  • A2: The whole decade series: the price across the decades of American shopping.
  • A3: The price against the whole economy: nominal and 2024 dollars side by side.

Key figures

  • points: 6
  • earliest_year: 1984
  • latest_year: 2024
  • per_lb_first: 1.29
  • per_lb_last: 5.39
  • per_lb_multiple: 4.18
  • per_lb_cagr: 0.0364

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1984, 1.29, 'per lb'), (1990, 1.59, 'per lb'), (2000, 1.57, 'per lb'), (2010, 2.37, 'per lb'), (2020, 4.12, 'per lb'), (2024, 5.39, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class GroundBeefPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1984, 1.29, 'per lb'), (1990, 1.59, 'per lb'), (2000, 1.57, 'per lb'), (2010, 2.37, 'per lb'), (2020, 4.12, 'per lb'), (2024, 5.39, 'per lb')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(GroundBeefPriceProgram().run())

Pound of Bacon, 1930-2024 JAH-COTY-000017 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A pound of sliced bacon is the most festive staple in the American price record: a breakfast meat whose price tracks the hog market, the feed market, and the fashion for bacon itself. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of sliced bacon per pound for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with each figure sourced, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Bacon is the database's best example of a price that falls in real terms for ninety years while the nominal price rises sixteenfold.

History

Sliced bacon cost forty-three cents a pound in 1930, fell to twenty-seven cents in the Depression year 1940, and rose to sixty-four cents in 1950 and sixty-six cents in 1960. The price passed a dollar in the 1970s, at ninety-five cents in 1970 and $1.46 in 1980, then doubled in the 1980s to $2.12 in 1990 and rose to $3.03 in 2000. The 2010s brought the bacon boom, as breakfast sandwiches and bacon-wrapped everything pushed demand up: $4.11 in 2010, $5.58 in 2020, and $6.78 in 2024, the highest in the record.

Description

A pound of sliced bacon is the most festive staple in the American price record: a breakfast meat whose price tracks the hog market, the feed market, and the fashion for bacon itself. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of sliced bacon per pound for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with each figure sourced, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Bacon is the database's best example of a price that falls in real terms for ninety years while the nominal price rises sixteenfold.

The Problem

What did a pound of sliced bacon cost in each decade from 1930 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was bacon genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1930 once inflation is removed?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average retail price of sliced bacon per pound from the Stacker compilation of BLS average prices, cross-checked against the MealFan grocery index. Step 2: record one anchor per decade, 1930 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1930: $0.43 per lb; 1940: $0.27 per lb; 1950: $0.64 per lb; 1960: $0.66 per lb; 1970: $0.95 per lb; 1980: $1.46 per lb; 1990: $2.12 per lb; 2000: $3.03 per lb; 2010: $4.11 per lb; 2020: $5.58 per lb; 2024: $6.78 per lb. The series has no missing decade anchors. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 11, "earliest_year": 1930, "latest_year": 2024, "per_lb_first": 0.43, "per_lb_last": 6.78, "per_lb_multiple": 15.77, "per_lb_cagr": 0.0298}.

The Answer

Bacon cost $0.43 a pound in 1930 and $6.78 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1930 pound cost about $8.08, so bacon became genuinely cheaper in real terms over ninety-four years, even though the nominal price rose nearly sixteenfold. The real price fell steadily until 2000 and then rose again as demand for bacon outran the hog supply. The series is 1930: $0.43 per lb; 1940: $0.27 per lb; 1950: $0.64 per lb; 1960: $0.66 per lb; 1970: $0.95 per lb; 1980: $1.46 per lb; 1990: $2.12 per lb; 2000: $3.03 per lb; 2010: $4.11 per lb; 2020: $5.58 per lb; 2024: $6.78 per lb. From 1930 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.43 to $6.78, a multiple of 15.77 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.98% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

The series has no missing decade anchors. The BLS pound is sliced bacon, the standard supermarket pack; thick-cut, center-cut, and artisan bacon cost far more and are not in this series. The 1930 and 1940 figures come from the early BLS retail-price program, whose city coverage was narrower than the modern survey. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1930 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.43 to $6.78, a multiple of 15.77 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.98% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Bacon prices follow hog prices, which follow corn and soybean prices, because feed is most of the cost of a pig. The long real decline reflects industrial hog farming: larger farms, formulated feed, and year-round production. The recent real rise reflects demand: bacon moved from a breakfast side to an ingredient in everything, and the hog supply did not keep up. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.43, 'per lb'), (1940, 0.27, 'per lb'), (1950, 0.64, 'per lb'), (1960, 0.66, 'per lb'), (1970, 0.95, 'per lb'), (1980, 1.46, 'per lb'), (1990, 2.12, 'per lb'), (2000, 3.03, 'per lb'), (2010, 4.11, 'per lb'), (2020, 5.58, 'per lb'), (2024, 6.78, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class BaconPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1930, 0.43, 'per lb'), (1940, 0.27, 'per lb'), (1950, 0.64, 'per lb'), (1960, 0.66, 'per lb'), (1970, 0.95, 'per lb'), (1980, 1.46, 'per lb'), (1990, 2.12, 'per lb'), (2000, 3.03, 'per lb'), (2010, 4.11, 'per lb'), (2020, 5.58, 'per lb'), (2024, 6.78, 'per lb')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(BaconPriceProgram().run())
