Pound of Sugar, 1950-2024 JAH-COTY-000018 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A pound of white sugar is the most stable price in the American record: it barely moved in nominal terms for twenty years, from 1980 to 2000, while nearly everything else doubled. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of white sugar per pound for 1950, 1970, and every decade from 1980 to 2024, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Sugar is the database's best example of a protected price: United States quotas and tariffs hold the domestic price above the world price and smooth its swings.

History

White sugar cost 10.2 cents a pound in 1950, according to BLS Bulletin 1055, and about twelve cents in 1970, when a five-pound bag sold for sixty cents. The price jumped in the mid-1970s, when the world sugar price spiked, and the BLS series begins in 1980 at forty-three cents a pound. The price then barely moved for twenty years: forty-three cents in 1990 and forty-two cents in 2000. It rose to sixty-three cents in 2010 and stayed there in 2020, then surged to one dollar in 2024, the first time the average pound of sugar cost a dollar.

Description

A pound of white sugar is the most stable price in the American record: it barely moved in nominal terms for twenty years, from 1980 to 2000, while nearly everything else doubled. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of white sugar per pound for 1950, 1970, and every decade from 1980 to 2024, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Sugar is the database's best example of a protected price: United States quotas and tariffs hold the domestic price above the world price and smooth its swings.

The Problem

What did a pound of white sugar cost in each decade from 1950 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was sugar genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1950 once inflation is removed?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average price of white sugar per pound from Pricele's BLS series APU0000715211, with the 1950 figure from BLS Bulletin 1055 and the 1970 figure from the CheddarDen household-price compilation. Step 2: record the 1950 and 1970 figures and one anchor per decade from 1980 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1950: $0.10 per lb; 1970: $0.12 per lb; 1980: $0.43 per lb; 1990: $0.43 per lb; 2000: $0.42 per lb; 2010: $0.63 per lb; 2020: $0.63 per lb; 2024: $1.00 per lb. Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS sugar series starts in 1980; the 1950 figure comes from BLS Bulletin 1055 and the 1970 figure from a household-price compilation; no sugar figure exists for 1930, 1940, or 1960 in the sources consulted. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 8, "earliest_year": 1950, "latest_year": 2024, "per_lb_first": 0.102, "per_lb_last": 1.0, "per_lb_multiple": 9.8, "per_lb_cagr": 0.0313}.

The Answer

Sugar cost about $0.10 a pound in 1950 and $1.00 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1950 pound cost about $1.33, so sugar became genuinely cheaper in real terms over seventy-four years, even after the 2022-2024 surge. The price was flat in nominal terms from 1980 to 2000, which means it fell by more than half in real terms over those twenty years. The series is 1950: $0.10 per lb; 1970: $0.12 per lb; 1980: $0.43 per lb; 1990: $0.43 per lb; 2000: $0.42 per lb; 2010: $0.63 per lb; 2020: $0.63 per lb; 2024: $1.00 per lb. From 1950 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.10 to $1.00, a multiple of 9.8 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.13% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS sugar series starts in 1980; the 1950 figure comes from BLS Bulletin 1055 and the 1970 figure from a household-price compilation; no sugar figure exists for 1930, 1940, or 1960 in the sources consulted. The BLS pound is white sugar, all sizes; the series is remarkably flat for decades because of the quota system, not because the world price was flat. The 1950 and 1970 figures come from older and compilation sources rather than the modern BLS series. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1950 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.10 to $1.00, a multiple of 9.8 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.13% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Sugar prices follow the world sugar market, but the United States sugar program holds the domestic price above the world price with quotas and tariffs, so the domestic price moves less than the world price. The 2022-2024 surge followed higher input costs for growers and refiners, and the domestic price rose with them. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

AI

The JAH Cost of Things Librarian answers questions about this record, explains the reasoning, and runs the demo and program on request.

  • BLS Bulletin 1055 (1952) - Retail Prices of Food, 1950 (mirror) — https://vitrine.hraedon.com/archive/sources/bls-bulletin-1055-1952.html (BLS Bulletin 1055: white sugar 10.2 cents per pound in 1950.)
  • MealFan - Grocery Price Index 2026 (BLS Average Price Data) — https://www.mealfan.com/grocery-price-index/ (BLS average price data for sugar, January 2020 and October 2026 points.)

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1950, 0.102, 'per lb'), (1970, 0.12, 'per lb'), (1980, 0.43, 'per lb'), (1990, 0.43, 'per lb'), (2000, 0.42, 'per lb'), (2010, 0.63, 'per lb'), (2020, 0.63, 'per lb'), (2024, 1.0, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Pound of Butter, 1950-2024 JAH-COTY-000019 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A pound of stick butter is the thinnest series in this database, and the record says so. The Bureau of Labor Statistics average-price series for butter, stick, per pound begins only in April 2018, so this record gives the 1950 figure from BLS Bulletin 1055 and the annual averages for 2018 through 2024, with the missing decades stated as gaps, never filled. Butter is the database's honest limit: a good record admits what it does not know.

History

Butter cost 72.9 cents a pound in 1950, according to BLS Bulletin 1055, and then the record goes quiet: the Bureau of Labor Statistics did not publish a stick-butter average price again until April 2018, when the series begins at $4.15 for the month and $4.07 for the year. The annual average was $3.98 in 2019, $3.62 in 2020, and $3.58 in 2021, then rose to $4.38 in 2022, $4.56 in 2023, and $4.74 in 2024, with the September 2024 monthly price reaching $5.00. This record states the gap plainly: it cannot show the butter prices of 1960, 1970, 1980, 1990, 2000, or 2010, because no BLS annual average exists for those years.

Description

A pound of stick butter is the thinnest series in this database, and the record says so. The Bureau of Labor Statistics average-price series for butter, stick, per pound begins only in April 2018, so this record gives the 1950 figure from BLS Bulletin 1055 and the annual averages for 2018 through 2024, with the missing decades stated as gaps, never filled. Butter is the database's honest limit: a good record admits what it does not know.

The Problem

What did a pound of butter cost in 1950 and in each year from 2018 to 2024, and was butter genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1950 once inflation is removed?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average price of stick butter per pound from the in2013dollars and USA Cost of Living compilations of the BLS average-price series, with the 1950 figure from BLS Bulletin 1055. Step 2: record the 1950 figure and the annual averages for 2018 through 2024, stating the missing decades as gaps. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages, using the CPI-U value of 103.9 for 1984-style back-computation only where the year is in the table, and the 1950 CPI-U of 24.1. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1950: $0.73 per lb; 2018: $4.07 per lb; 2019: $3.98 per lb; 2020: $3.62 per lb; 2021: $3.58 per lb; 2022: $4.38 per lb; 2023: $4.56 per lb; 2024: $4.74 per lb. Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS stick-butter average-price series starts in April 2018, so no annual averages exist for 1960, 1970, 1980, 1990, 2000, or 2010; the 1950 figure comes from BLS Bulletin 1055; a compilation page lists butter figures for 1980-2010, but they conflict with the BLS stick-butter annual averages for the overlapping years, so they are not used. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 8, "earliest_year": 1950, "latest_year": 2024, "per_lb_first": 0.729, "per_lb_last": 4.74, "per_lb_multiple": 6.5, "per_lb_cagr": 0.0256}.

The Answer

Butter cost $0.73 a pound in 1950 and $4.74 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1950 pound cost about $9.49, so butter became much cheaper in real terms over seventy-four years, even after the 2022-2024 rise. The stick-butter average was $4.07 in 2018, fell to $3.58 in 2021, and rose to $4.74 in 2024. The series is 1950: $0.73 per lb; 2018: $4.07 per lb; 2019: $3.98 per lb; 2020: $3.62 per lb; 2021: $3.58 per lb; 2022: $4.38 per lb; 2023: $4.56 per lb; 2024: $4.74 per lb. From 1950 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.73 to $4.74, a multiple of 6.5 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.56% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS stick-butter average-price series starts in April 2018, so no annual averages exist for 1960, 1970, 1980, 1990, 2000, or 2010; the 1950 figure comes from BLS Bulletin 1055; a compilation page lists butter figures for 1980-2010, but they conflict with the BLS stick-butter annual averages for the overlapping years, so they are not used. The long gap means this record cannot show the 1960-2010 path, and no figure here should be read as the whole story. A compilation page lists butter figures for 1980-2010, but they conflict with the BLS stick-butter annual averages for the overlapping years, so they are not used in this record. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1950 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.73 to $4.74, a multiple of 6.5 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.56% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Butter prices follow milk prices, feed costs, and the butterfat market: when milk is dear, butter is dear, and when feed is dear, milk is dear. The 2022 rise followed the same feed and energy shocks that lifted milk and eggs, and the September 2024 monthly price of $5.00 was the highest in the short series. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Sources

History

Butter cost 72.9 cents a pound in 1950, according to BLS Bulletin 1055, and then the record goes quiet: the Bureau of Labor Statistics did not publish a stick-butter average price again until April 2018, when the series begins at $4.15 for the month and $4.07 for the year. The annual average was $3.98 in 2019, $3.62 in 2020, and $3.58 in 2021, then rose to $4.38 in 2022, $4.56 in 2023, and $4.74 in 2024, with the September 2024 monthly price reaching $5.00. This record states the gap plainly: it cannot show the butter prices of 1960, 1970, 1980, 1990, 2000, or 2010, because no BLS annual average exists for those years.

Problem

What did a pound of butter cost in 1950 and in each year from 2018 to 2024, and was butter genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1950 once inflation is removed?

Method

Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS stick-butter average-price series starts in April 2018, so no annual averages exist for 1960, 1970, 1980, 1990, 2000, or 2010; the 1950 figure comes from BLS Bulletin 1055; a compilation page lists butter figures for 1980-2010, but they conflict with the BLS stick-butter annual averages for the overlapping years, so they are not used. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars.

  • care: Never present a gap year as a price; never mix units across a unit change; always state the unit and the year with every figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1950, 0.729, 'per lb'), (2018, 4.07, 'per lb'), (2019, 3.98, 'per lb'), (2020, 3.62, 'per lb'), (2021, 3.58, 'per lb'), (2022, 4.38, 'per lb'), (2023, 4.56, 'per lb'), (2024, 4.74, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ButterPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1950, 0.729, 'per lb'), (2018, 4.07, 'per lb'), (2019, 3.98, 'per lb'), (2020, 3.62, 'per lb'), (2021, 3.58, 'per lb'), (2022, 4.38, 'per lb'), (2023, 4.56, 'per lb'), (2024, 4.74, 'per lb')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ButterPriceProgram().run())

Pound of Cheddar Cheese, 1970-2024 JAH-COTY-000020 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A pound of natural cheddar cheese is the dairy case's middle child: dearer than processed cheese, cheaper than aged imports, and priced by the Bureau of Labor Statistics since 1984. This record gives the BLS average retail price of natural cheddar per pound for 1984, 2000, 2010, 2020, and 2024, with the 1970 compilation figure as the earliest anchor, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Cheddar is the database's steadiest dairy price: it rose more slowly than general inflation for forty years.

History

Natural cheddar cost seventy-seven cents a pound in 1970, according to the CheddarDen household-price compilation. The Bureau of Labor Statistics series begins in 1984, when cheddar averaged $3.07 a pound, and the price rose to $3.83 in 2000, $4.71 in 2010, $5.50 in 2020, and $5.66 in 2024. The sharpest single jump came in 2011, when the annual average rose 15 percent to $5.42, and the lowest annual average of the modern era was $4.90 in 2017. The September 2026 monthly price reached $6.13, the highest the BLS has recorded.

Description

A pound of natural cheddar cheese is the dairy case's middle child: dearer than processed cheese, cheaper than aged imports, and priced by the Bureau of Labor Statistics since 1984. This record gives the BLS average retail price of natural cheddar per pound for 1984, 2000, 2010, 2020, and 2024, with the 1970 compilation figure as the earliest anchor, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Cheddar is the database's steadiest dairy price: it rose more slowly than general inflation for forty years.

The Problem

What did a pound of natural cheddar cheese cost in 1970, 1984, and each decade from 2000 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was cheddar genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1970 once inflation is removed?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average price of natural cheddar cheese per pound from Pricele's BLS series APU0000710212, cross-checked against the BLS monthly table, with the 1970 figure from the CheddarDen household-price compilation. Step 2: record the 1970 figure, the 1984 starting figure, and one anchor per decade from 2000 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1970: $0.77 per lb; 1984: $3.07 per lb; 2000: $3.83 per lb; 2010: $4.71 per lb; 2020: $5.50 per lb; 2024: $5.66 per lb. Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS cheddar series starts in 1984, so no annual average exists before that year except the 1970 compilation figure; BLS published fewer than ten months of cheddar prices in 1989 and 1990, so no 1990 annual average exists in the sources consulted. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 6, "earliest_year": 1970, "latest_year": 2024, "per_lb_first": 0.77, "per_lb_last": 5.66, "per_lb_multiple": 7.35, "per_lb_cagr": 0.0376}.

The Answer

Cheddar cost $0.77 a pound in 1970 and $5.66 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1970 pound cost about $6.23, so cheddar became slightly cheaper in real terms over fifty-four years, even though the nominal price rose more than sevenfold. The 1984 pound cost about $9.27 in 2024 dollars, so cheddar has become much cheaper in real terms since the series began. The series is 1970: $0.77 per lb; 1984: $3.07 per lb; 2000: $3.83 per lb; 2010: $4.71 per lb; 2020: $5.50 per lb; 2024: $5.66 per lb. From 1970 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.77 to $5.66, a multiple of 7.35 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.76% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS cheddar series starts in 1984, so no annual average exists before that year except the 1970 compilation figure; BLS published fewer than ten months of cheddar prices in 1989 and 1990, so no 1990 annual average exists in the sources consulted. Natural cheddar only; processed cheese is cheaper and is not in this series. The 1970 figure is a compilation figure, not a BLS average, and no 1990 annual average exists because BLS published fewer than ten months of cheddar prices in 1989 and 1990. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. Cheddar prices follow milk prices and aging costs: a pound of cheddar takes about ten pounds of milk, and the cheese must be stored and aged, so the price moves with the milk market and with energy costs for refrigeration. The 2011 jump followed a milk-price spike, and the 2017 low followed a milk surplus. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The Bureau of Labor Statistics series begins in 1984, when cheddar averaged $3.07 a pound, and the price rose to $3.83 in 2000, $4.71 in 2010, $5.50 in 2020, and $5.66 in 2024. The sharpest single jump came in 2011, when the annual average rose 15 percent to $5.42, and the lowest annual average of the modern era was $4.90 in 2017. The September 2026 monthly price reached $6.13, the highest the BLS has recorded.

Problem

What did a pound of natural cheddar cheese cost in 1970, 1984, and each decade from 2000 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was cheddar genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1970 once inflation is removed?

Method

Step 1: take the BLS average price of natural cheddar cheese per pound from Pricele's BLS series APU0000710212, cross-checked against the BLS monthly table, with the 1970 figure from the CheddarDen household-price compilation. Step 2: record the 1970 figure, the 1984 starting figure, and one anchor per decade from 2000 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1970: $0.77 per lb; 1984: $3.07 per lb; 2000: $3.83 per lb; 2010: $4.71 per lb; 2020: $5.50 per lb; 2024: $5.66 per lb. Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS cheddar series starts in 1984, so no annual average exists before that year except the 1970 compilation figure; BLS published fewer than ten months of cheddar prices in 1989 and 1990, so no 1990 annual average exists in the sources consulted. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars.

Dataset

BLS average retail prices and CPI-U annual averages as cited; series years 1970-2024; CPI-U 2024 annual average 313.7.

Verification path

Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Formal equation

P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1

Formal equation, in plain words

P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

Cheddar cost $0.77 a pound in 1970 and $5.66 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1970 pound cost about $6.23, so cheddar became slightly cheaper in real terms over fifty-four years, even though the nominal price rose more than sevenfold. The 1984 pound cost about $9.27 in 2024 dollars, so cheddar has become much cheaper in real terms since the series began. The series is 1970: $0.77 per lb; 1984: $3.07 per lb; 2000: $3.83 per lb; 2010: $4.71 per lb; 2020: $5.50 per lb; 2024: $5.66 per lb. From 1970 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.77 to $5.66, a multiple of 7.35 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.76% per year.

Skeptical analysis

Gaps stated, not filled: the BLS cheddar series starts in 1984, so no annual average exists before that year except the 1970 compilation figure; BLS published fewer than ten months of cheddar prices in 1989 and 1990, so no 1990 annual average exists in the sources consulted. Natural cheddar only; processed cheese is cheaper and is not in this series. The 1970 figure is a compilation figure, not a BLS average, and no 1990 annual average exists because BLS published fewer than ten months of cheddar prices in 1989 and 1990. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Specification

  • dimensions: price in US dollars per per lb; calendar years 1970-2024; CPI-U index points
  • care: Never present a gap year as a price; never mix units across a unit change; always state the unit and the year with every figure.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One pound of natural cheddar cheese in one year: what it cost and what that meant.
  • A2: The whole decade series: the price across the decades of American shopping.
  • A3: The price against the whole economy: nominal and 2024 dollars side by side.

Key figures

  • points: 6
  • earliest_year: 1970
  • latest_year: 2024
  • per_lb_first: 0.77
  • per_lb_last: 5.66
  • per_lb_multiple: 7.35
  • per_lb_cagr: 0.0376

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1970, 0.77, 'per lb'), (1984, 3.07, 'per lb'), (2000, 3.83, 'per lb'), (2010, 4.71, 'per lb'), (2020, 5.5, 'per lb'), (2024, 5.66, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class CheddarPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1970, 0.77, 'per lb'), (1984, 3.07, 'per lb'), (2000, 3.83, 'per lb'), (2010, 4.71, 'per lb'), (2020, 5.5, 'per lb'), (2024, 5.66, 'per lb')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(CheddarPriceProgram().run())

Candy Bar (Standard Hershey Bar), 1930-2024 JAH-COTY-000021 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A standard Hershey milk chocolate bar is the most famous five-cent item in American history, and its price is the database's best lesson in shrinkflation: the company held the nickel price for forty-eight years by making the bar smaller, and only then raised the price. This record gives the standard bar's price for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with the later figures labeled as the approximations and ranges they are, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. The bar cost five cents in 1930, 1940, 1950, and 1960, and the record says so four times, because that flat line is the story.

History

The Hershey milk chocolate bar cost five cents from 1921 until November 24, 1969, when the company doubled the bar's weight to one and a half ounces and raised the price to ten cents. The bar had been shrinking for decades to hold the nickel price: two ounces in 1930, one ounce in 1960, three-quarters of an ounce in 1968. After 1969 the price rose in steps: fifteen cents in 1974, twenty cents in 1977, twenty-five cents in 1978, thirty cents in 1982, thirty-five cents in 1983, and forty cents in 1986. The average reached about forty-five cents around 1991 and about seventy-five cents in the late 1990s, and a standard bar sells for about two dollars in the 2020s.

Description

A standard Hershey milk chocolate bar is the most famous five-cent item in American history, and its price is the database's best lesson in shrinkflation: the company held the nickel price for forty-eight years by making the bar smaller, and only then raised the price. This record gives the standard bar's price for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with the later figures labeled as the approximations and ranges they are, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. The bar cost five cents in 1930, 1940, 1950, and 1960, and the record says so four times, because that flat line is the story.

The Problem

What did a standard candy bar cost in each decade from 1930 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and how much of the rise is the bar getting smaller rather than dearer?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the standard Hershey bar price by year from the Answers.com price-and-weight table and the Encyclopedia.com company history, cross-checked against Snack History. Step 2: record one anchor per decade, 1930 through 2024, labeling the 1990, 2000, and 2024 figures as the approximations and ranges they are. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span, and report the weight change alongside the price change. The series is 1930: $0.05 each; 1940: $0.05 each; 1950: $0.05 each; 1960: $0.05 each; 1970: $0.10 each; 1980: $0.25 each; 1990: $0.45 each; 2000: $0.75 each; 2024: $2.00 each. Gaps stated, not filled: no solid national figure exists for 2010, so the 2010 anchor is stated as a gap; the 1990 figure of $0.45 is the average reported for 1991, used as the 1990 anchor and labeled as such; the 2000 figure of $0.75 is an approximate late-1990s retail price, labeled as such; the 2024 figure of $2.00 is the midpoint of the $1.50-$2.50 typical single-bar retail range reported for the 2020s, labeled as such; the bar shrank over the decades: the nickel bar weighed 2 ounces in 1930 and 3/4 ounce in 1968, so the price per ounce rose far faster than the price per bar. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 9, "earliest_year": 1930, "latest_year": 2024, "each_first": 0.05, "each_last": 2.0, "each_multiple": 40.0, "each_cagr": 0.04}.

The Answer

A standard candy bar cost $0.05 in 1930 and about $2.00 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1930 bar cost about $0.94, so the bar became genuinely dearer in real terms, roughly doubling. The price per ounce rose far faster, because the bar shrank from 2 ounces in 1930 to about 1.5 ounces today. The series is 1930: $0.05 each; 1940: $0.05 each; 1950: $0.05 each; 1960: $0.05 each; 1970: $0.10 each; 1980: $0.25 each; 1990: $0.45 each; 2000: $0.75 each; 2024: $2.00 each. From 1930 to 2024 the each price rose from $0.05 to $2.00, a multiple of 40.0 and a compound annual growth rate of 4.0% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: no solid national figure exists for 2010, so the 2010 anchor is stated as a gap; the 1990 figure of $0.45 is the average reported for 1991, used as the 1990 anchor and labeled as such; the 2000 figure of $0.75 is an approximate late-1990s retail price, labeled as such; the 2024 figure of $2.00 is the midpoint of the $1.50-$2.50 typical single-bar retail range reported for the 2020s, labeled as such; the bar shrank over the decades: the nickel bar weighed 2 ounces in 1930 and 3/4 ounce in 1968, so the price per ounce rose far faster than the price per bar. The standard bar only; king-size and miniature bars differ. The 1990, 2000, and 2024 figures are labeled approximations and ranges, not BLS averages, and no solid national figure exists for 2010. The price per ounce, not the price per bar, is the honest measure of what chocolate cost. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1930 to 2024 the each price rose from $0.05 to $2.00, a multiple of 40.0 and a compound annual growth rate of 4.0% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Shrinkflation is the mechanism: Hershey held the nickel price for forty-eight years by shrinking the bar, because a five-cent coin was the unit of the vending machine and the corner store. Cocoa, sugar, and milk set the floor under the price, and when those rose faster than the bar could shrink, the price had to rise in steps. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

AI

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.05, 'each'), (1940, 0.05, 'each'), (1950, 0.05, 'each'), (1960, 0.05, 'each'), (1970, 0.1, 'each'), (1980, 0.25, 'each'), (1990, 0.45, 'each'), (2000, 0.75, 'each'), (2024, 2.0, 'each')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class CandyBarPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1930, 0.05, 'each'), (1940, 0.05, 'each'), (1950, 0.05, 'each'), (1960, 0.05, 'each'), (1970, 0.1, 'each'), (1980, 0.25, 'each'), (1990, 0.45, 'each'), (2000, 0.75, 'each'), (2024, 2.0, 'each')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(CandyBarPriceProgram().run())

Bottle of Coca-Cola, 1930-2013 JAH-COTY-000022 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A bottle of Coca-Cola is the most famous fixed price in economic history: seventy-three years at a nickel, the longest documented case of a price not changing. This record gives the price of a 6.5-ounce glass bottle for 1930, 1940, 1950, 1960, and 1970, and the price of a 2-liter bottle for 2002, 2005, 2011, 2012, and 2013, keeping the two series strictly apart and converting every price into 2024 dollars within its own series. The nickel Coke is the database's proof that prices do not always rise: sometimes they are pinned in place by contracts and machines.

History

A 6.5-ounce glass bottle of Coca-Cola cost five cents from 1886 until 1959, seventy-three years at the same nickel price, through two world wars, the Prohibition era, and the Great Depression. The price held because of three locks: the 1899 bottling contracts, which fixed the syrup price and encouraged retail price maintenance; the vending machines, which were built to take a single nickel; and the advertising, which painted Drink Coca-Cola, 5 Cents on buildings across the country. In the 1950s the fixed price was phased out, local prices rose to between six and ten cents, and the last nickel Coke was sold in 1959. The bottle cost about ten cents in 1960 and fifteen cents in 1970. The modern 2-liter bottle cost $0.99 in 2002, $1.09 in 2005, $1.89 in 2011 and 2012, and $1.99 in 2013.

Description

A bottle of Coca-Cola is the most famous fixed price in economic history: seventy-three years at a nickel, the longest documented case of a price not changing. This record gives the price of a 6.5-ounce glass bottle for 1930, 1940, 1950, 1960, and 1970, and the price of a 2-liter bottle for 2002, 2005, 2011, 2012, and 2013, keeping the two series strictly apart and converting every price into 2024 dollars within its own series. The nickel Coke is the database's proof that prices do not always rise: sometimes they are pinned in place by contracts and machines.

The Problem

What did a bottle of Coca-Cola cost in each decade from 1930 to 2013, how did the nickel price hold for seventy-three years, and what happened to the price once the fixed price ended?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the fixed nickel price and its end from Wikipedia's fixed-price article and the North Country Public Radio account of Levy and Young's 2004 study, and the 2-liter prices from the Food Timeline compilation. Step 2: record the glass-bottle figures for 1930, 1940, 1950, 1960, and 1970, and the 2-liter figures for 2002, 2005, 2011, 2012, and 2013. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate within each series, and never compute a multiple across the two series. The series is 1930: $0.05 glass bottle; 1940: $0.05 glass bottle; 1950: $0.05 glass bottle; 1960: $0.10 glass bottle; 1970: $0.15 glass bottle; 2002: $0.99 2-liter bottle; 2005: $1.09 2-liter bottle; 2011: $1.89 2-liter bottle; 2012: $1.89 2-liter bottle; 2013: $1.99 2-liter bottle. Two series appear here and must not be mixed: the 6.5-ounce glass bottle (1930-1970) and the 2-liter bottle (2002-2013). The demo computes multiples only within each series. Gaps stated, not filled: the nickel price held from 1886 to 1959, so the 1930, 1940, and 1950 bottle figures are all five cents by the same fixed price; the 1960 and 1970 bottle figures are typical retail prices from the period after the fixed price ended, labeled as such; no 2-liter figure exists for 1980, 1990, 2000, 2020, or 2024 in the sources consulted; later single-serve prices are typical-retail compilations, not a federal series, and are described in the record text rather than plotted as series points. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 10, "earliest_year": 1930, "latest_year": 2013, "glass_bottle_first": 0.05, "glass_bottle_last": 0.15, "glass_bottle_multiple": 3.0, "glass_bottle_cagr": 0.0278, "2_liter_bottle_first": 0.99, "2_liter_bottle_last": 1.99, "2_liter_bottle_multiple": 2.01, "2_liter_bottle_cagr": 0.0655}.

The Answer

The glass bottle cost $0.05 in 1930, 1940, and 1950, then $0.10 in 1960 and $0.15 in 1970. In 2024 dollars the 1930 nickel equaled about $0.94, so the nickel Coke became six times cheaper in real terms before the fixed price ended. The 2-liter bottle cost $0.99 in 2002 and $1.99 in 2013, doubling in eleven years. The series is 1930: $0.05 glass bottle; 1940: $0.05 glass bottle; 1950: $0.05 glass bottle; 1960: $0.10 glass bottle; 1970: $0.15 glass bottle; 2002: $0.99 2-liter bottle; 2005: $1.09 2-liter bottle; 2011: $1.89 2-liter bottle; 2012: $1.89 2-liter bottle; 2013: $1.99 2-liter bottle. From 1930 to 1970 the glass bottle price rose from $0.05 to $0.15, a multiple of 3.0 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.78% per year. From 2002 to 2013 the 2-liter bottle price rose from $0.99 to $1.99, a multiple of 2.01 and a compound annual growth rate of 6.55% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

Gaps stated, not filled: the nickel price held from 1886 to 1959, so the 1930, 1940, and 1950 bottle figures are all five cents by the same fixed price; the 1960 and 1970 bottle figures are typical retail prices from the period after the fixed price ended, labeled as such; no 2-liter figure exists for 1980, 1990, 2000, 2020, or 2024 in the sources consulted; later single-serve prices are typical-retail compilations, not a federal series, and are described in the record text rather than plotted as series points. The later bottle figures are typical retail prices, not a national average, and the 2-liter series is short and ends in 2013. Later single-serve prices are typical-retail compilations, not a federal series, and they are described in the record text rather than plotted as series points. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1930 to 1970 the glass bottle price rose from $0.05 to $0.15, a multiple of 3.0 and a compound annual growth rate of 2.78% per year. From 2002 to 2013 the 2-liter bottle price rose from $0.99 to $1.99, a multiple of 2.01 and a compound annual growth rate of 6.55% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Nominal price rigidity is the mechanism: the bottler contracts, the single-coin vending machines, and the advertising that printed the price all made a rise costly, and the company asked the Treasury to mint a 7.5-cent coin rather than raise the price. Inflation finally broke the nickel, because the cost of sugar, glass, and labor rose while the price could not. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.05, 'glass bottle'), (1940, 0.05, 'glass bottle'), (1950, 0.05, 'glass bottle'), (1960, 0.1, 'glass bottle'), (1970, 0.15, 'glass bottle'), (2002, 0.99, '2-liter bottle'), (2005, 1.09, '2-liter bottle'), (2011, 1.89, '2-liter bottle'), (2012, 1.89, '2-liter bottle'), (2013, 1.99, '2-liter bottle')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class CocaColaPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1930, 0.05, 'glass bottle'), (1940, 0.05, 'glass bottle'), (1950, 0.05, 'glass bottle'), (1960, 0.1, 'glass bottle'), (1970, 0.15, 'glass bottle'), (2002, 0.99, '2-liter bottle'), (2005, 1.09, '2-liter bottle'), (2011, 1.89, '2-liter bottle'), (2012, 1.89, '2-liter bottle'), (2013, 1.99, '2-liter bottle')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(CocaColaPriceProgram().run())

Pound of Round Steak, 1930-2024 JAH-COTY-000023 · compounded

Evidence: established Each price figure is taken from the cited public sources with its year; gaps are stated, not filled.

A pound of round steak is the Sunday roast of the American price record: a lean beef cut whose price tracks the cattle market, the feed market, and the American appetite for beef. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of round steak per pound for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with each figure sourced, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Round steak is the database's most perfectly inflation-matched price: it cost almost exactly the same in real terms in 2024 as in 1930.

History

Round steak cost forty-three cents a pound in 1930, fell to thirty-six cents in the Depression year 1940, and rose to ninety-four cents in 1950 and $1.06 in 1960. The price passed two dollars in the 1970s, at $1.30 in 1970 and $2.77 in 1980, and reached $3.32 in 1990 and $3.24 in 2000. The 2010s brought the beef boom: $4.28 in 2010, $6.53 in 2020, and $8.10 in 2024, the highest in the record. Round steak replaced the daily newspaper in this database, because no reliable decade-by-decade national series exists for a newspaper price, while round steak carries a full BLS series; the swap is recorded in the wave report.

Description

A pound of round steak is the Sunday roast of the American price record: a lean beef cut whose price tracks the cattle market, the feed market, and the American appetite for beef. This record gives the Bureau of Labor Statistics average retail price of round steak per pound for every decade from 1930 to 2024, with each figure sourced, and converts every price into 2024 dollars. Round steak is the database's most perfectly inflation-matched price: it cost almost exactly the same in real terms in 2024 as in 1930.

The Problem

What did a pound of round steak cost in each decade from 1930 to 2024, how fast did the price rise, and was steak genuinely dearer in 2024 than in 1930 once inflation is removed?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the BLS average retail price of round steak per pound from the Stacker compilation of BLS average prices, cross-checked against BLS Bulletin 1055 for 1950. Step 2: record one anchor per decade, 1930 through 2024. Step 3: convert each price into 2024 dollars with the CPI-U annual averages. Step 4: compute the nominal multiple and compound annual growth rate over the whole span. The series is 1930: $0.43 per lb; 1940: $0.36 per lb; 1950: $0.94 per lb; 1960: $1.06 per lb; 1970: $1.30 per lb; 1980: $2.77 per lb; 1990: $3.32 per lb; 2000: $3.24 per lb; 2010: $4.28 per lb; 2020: $6.53 per lb; 2024: $8.10 per lb. The series has no missing decade anchors. Each price is also read against the CPI-U annual average for its year (1930 16.7; 1940 14.0; 1950 24.1; 1960 29.6; 1970 38.8; 1980 82.4; 1990 130.7; 2000 172.2; 2010 218.1; 2020 258.8; 2024 313.7), so the demo converts every decade price into 2024 dollars. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"points": 11, "earliest_year": 1930, "latest_year": 2024, "per_lb_first": 0.43, "per_lb_last": 8.1, "per_lb_multiple": 18.84, "per_lb_cagr": 0.0317}.

The Answer

Round steak cost $0.43 a pound in 1930 and $8.10 in 2024. In 2024 dollars the 1930 pound cost about $8.08, so steak cost almost exactly the same in real terms in 2024 as in 1930, even though the nominal price rose nearly nineteenfold. The real price fell through the 1990s and 2000s and then rose sharply after 2010 as the cattle herd shrank. The series is 1930: $0.43 per lb; 1940: $0.36 per lb; 1950: $0.94 per lb; 1960: $1.06 per lb; 1970: $1.30 per lb; 1980: $2.77 per lb; 1990: $3.32 per lb; 2000: $3.24 per lb; 2010: $4.28 per lb; 2020: $6.53 per lb; 2024: $8.10 per lb. From 1930 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.43 to $8.10, a multiple of 18.84 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.17% per year.

When It Applies

When comparing what everyday things cost in different decades, when teaching inflation, or when checking a family story about prices against the published record.

Limits

The series has no missing decade anchors. Round steak is one cut; ribeye and sirloin cost far more and are not in this series. The 1950 figure of 93.6 cents comes from BLS Bulletin 1055, and the 1930 and 1940 figures come from the early BLS retail-price program, whose city coverage was narrower than the modern survey. National averages hide regional variation, quality change, and package-size change; a pound, a gallon, or a ticket in 1930 is not the same product as in 2024, and the figures here are averages, not what any one shopper paid.

Math Proof

The nominal multiple is M = P_last / P_first. The compound annual growth rate over N years is CAGR = M^(1/N) - 1. A price in 2024 dollars is P_2024 = P_year x 313.7 / CPI_year, where 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024. From 1930 to 2024 the per lb price rose from $0.43 to $8.10, a multiple of 18.84 and a compound annual growth rate of 3.17% per year. The demo recomputes all three from the key figures and prints them twice with SHA-256 digests. Formal statement: P_2024 = P_year * 313.7 / CPI_year; CAGR = (P_last / P_first)^(1/N) - 1. P_year is the nominal price in the stated year and unit; CPI_year is the CPI-U annual average for that year (1982-84 = 100); 313.7 is the CPI-U annual average for 2024; N is the number of years between the first and last price in the same unit.

Science Proof

The Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U) tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a basket of goods and services. Dividing a past price by the CPI-U of its year and multiplying by the CPI-U of 2024 (313.7) restates that price in 2024 dollars, so a reader can tell whether the item genuinely became more expensive or only kept up with general inflation. A nominal rise that matches the CPI-U rise means the item held its real price; a rise above it means the item became genuinely dearer. Beef prices follow the cattle cycle, like ground beef: ranchers expand the herd when calf prices are high and cull it when feed is dear, and the cycle runs about ten years. Round steak is a lean cut, so its price tracks the whole carcass, and the 2014-2015 drought and the 2020-2024 herd shrinkage are both visible in this series. Scientific relation: CPI-U annual average, all items, U.S. city average, not seasonally adjusted, base 1982-84 = 100. The demo recomputes the nominal multiple, the compound annual growth rate, and the 2024-dollar conversion for every decade price from the key figures. The cited sources confirm the price figures and the CPI-U values.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. Re-check the cited sources: each URL must return HTTP 200, and each price figure must match the year and source named in the record.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

A seeded boundless generator for this record can produce further instances on demand, up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file; outputs are generated, not stored.

AI

The JAH Cost of Things Librarian answers questions about this record, explains the reasoning, and runs the demo and program on request.

  • BLS Bulletin 1055 (1952) - Retail Prices of Food, 1950 (mirror) — https://vitrine.hraedon.com/archive/sources/bls-bulletin-1055-1952.html (BLS Bulletin 1055: round steak 93.6 cents per pound in 1950.)

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
series = [(1930, 0.43, 'per lb'), (1940, 0.36, 'per lb'), (1950, 0.94, 'per lb'), (1960, 1.06, 'per lb'), (1970, 1.3, 'per lb'), (1980, 2.77, 'per lb'), (1990, 3.32, 'per lb'), (2000, 3.24, 'per lb'), (2010, 4.28, 'per lb'), (2020, 6.53, 'per lb'), (2024, 8.1, 'per lb')]
cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
def compute():
    groups = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
    out = {}
    for u in sorted(groups):
        pts = groups[u]
        y0, p0 = pts[0]
        y1, p1 = pts[-1]
        key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
        out[key + '_first'] = p0
        out[key + '_last'] = p1
        out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
        out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
        out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
    conv = {}
    for (y, p, u) in series:
        if y in cpi:
            conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
    out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
    out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
    out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
    out['points'] = len(series)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class RoundSteakPriceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        series = [(1930, 0.43, 'per lb'), (1940, 0.36, 'per lb'), (1950, 0.94, 'per lb'), (1960, 1.06, 'per lb'), (1970, 1.3, 'per lb'), (1980, 2.77, 'per lb'), (1990, 3.32, 'per lb'), (2000, 3.24, 'per lb'), (2010, 4.28, 'per lb'), (2020, 6.53, 'per lb'), (2024, 8.1, 'per lb')]
        cpi = {1930: 16.7, 1940: 14.0, 1950: 24.1, 1960: 29.6, 1970: 38.8, 1980: 82.4, 1990: 130.7, 2000: 172.2, 2010: 218.1, 2020: 258.8, 2024: 313.7}
        def compute():
            groups = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                groups.setdefault(u, []).append((y, p))
            out = {}
            for u in sorted(groups):
                pts = groups[u]
                y0, p0 = pts[0]
                y1, p1 = pts[-1]
                key = u.replace(' ', '_').replace('-', '_').replace('/', '_').replace('.', '')
                out[key + '_first'] = p0
                out[key + '_last'] = p1
                out[key + '_multiple'] = round(p1 / p0, 2)
                out[key + '_span_years'] = y1 - y0
                out[key + '_cagr'] = round((p1 / p0) ** (1.0 / (y1 - y0)) - 1.0, 4) if y1 > y0 else 0.0
            conv = {}
            for (y, p, u) in series:
                if y in cpi:
                    conv[y] = round(p * 313.7 / cpi[y], 2)
            out['in_2024_dollars'] = conv
            out['earliest_year'] = series[0][0]
            out['latest_year'] = series[-1][0]
            out['points'] = len(series)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(RoundSteakPriceProgram().run())
